Then with the automatic instinct for self-preservation he sprang back into the bushes, and began to push his way through them until he had reached a safe distance from the danger zone.

James, the cat, meanwhile, hurt at the manner in which his friendly move had been received, had taken refuge on the outhouse roof. He mewed complainingly, a puzzled note in his voice. Mr. Pickering’s behavior had been one of those things that no fellow can understand. The whole thing seemed inexplicable to James.

===Chinese===

可他没有工夫捉摸。机会就在眼前，是他此生唯一一次得窥那座棚屋、探明其中神秘内容的机会。他曾亲眼见那姑娘开了锁。几步路就到。缺的只是胆量。皮克林先生奋力一搏，总算把胆量弄到了手。他从灌木中暴起，一溜小跑奔到棚屋门前，拉开门朝里望去。就在这一刻，有个东西碰了他的腿。

在恰当的时机、怀着恰当的心境，人能做出令人惊叹叹服的斯多噶式坚忍。皮克林先生绝非孱弱之辈。有一回他的汽车翻进了沟里，他守着一条断了的手臂等救援，足足等了二十分钟，自始至终没哼一声。可眼前这一回，情形不同。他的心思没有调校到应付这桩变故的档位上。有些时候，碰一碰一个人的腿是不合时宜的。此刻之于皮克林先生，正是这种不合时宜的时候。他无声地腾身跃起，整个身心像遭了天崩地裂，被生生撕作两半。

他手里一直攥着那把左轮手枪，用以防备无名的恐怖；跃起之际，他扣动了扳机。随后，凭着一股自动自发的求生本能，他又窜回灌木丛，一路披枝斩叶往外拱，直到离险境有了安全的距离。

至于那只猫詹姆斯，自己一番友好的举动竟得到这般接待，它深感受了伤，已逃上棚屋顶避难去了。它呜呜喵喵地抱怨着，嗓音里带着一团困惑。皮克林先生的行径，属于那种“谁也没法弄懂”的事情。整桩事情，在詹姆斯看来全然不可索解。

## XVIII / 第十八章

===Original===
Lord Dawlish stood in the doorway of the outhouse, holding the body of Eustace gingerly by the tail. It was a solemn moment. There was no room for doubt as to the completeness of the extinction of Lady Wetherby’s pet. Dudley Pickering’s bullet had done its lethal work. Eustace’s adventurous career was over. He was through.
Elizabeth’s mouth was trembling and she looked very white in the moonlight. Being naturally softhearted, she deplored the tragedy for its own sake; and she was also, though not lacking in courage, decidedly upset by the discovery that some person unknown had been roaming her premises with a firearm.
“Oh, Bill!” she said. Then: “Poor little chap!” And then: “Who could have done it?”
Lord Dawlish did not answer. His whole mind was occupied at the moment with the contemplation of the fact that she had called him Bill. Then he realized that she had spoken three times and expected a reply.
“Who could have done it?”

===Chinese===
达利什勋爵站在棚屋门口，小心翼翼地拎着尾巴，提着尤斯塔斯的尸身。这是个肃穆的时刻。韦瑟比夫人的爱宠已彻底毙命，这一点无可怀疑。达德利·皮克林的子弹干完了它那要命的营生。尤斯塔斯冒险的一生就此落幕。它完蛋了。
伊丽莎白嘴唇发颤，月光下脸色煞白。她天生一副软心肠，单为这桩惨事本身便已痛惜；何况她还发觉，竟有个来历不明的人揣着火器在她的地界里游荡——她胆气虽壮，此刻也着实慌了神。
“哦，比尔！”她说。跟着又道：“可怜的小家伙！”再跟着：“这是谁干的？”
达利什勋爵没有答话。他此刻全副心思都用在咀嚼一桩事实上：她叫了他比尔。随后他才醒悟，她已经说了三遍话，正等着回音。
“这是谁干的？”

===Original===
Bill pondered. Never a quick thinker, the question found him unprepared.
“Some fellow, I expect,” he said at last brightly. “Got in, don’t you know, and then his pistol went off by accident.”
“But what was he doing with a pistol?”
Bill looked a little puzzled at this.
“Why, he would have a pistol, wouldn’t he? I thought everybody had over here.”
Except for what he had been able to observe during the brief period of his present visit, Lord Dawlish’s knowledge of the United States had been derived from the American plays which he had seen in London, and in these chappies were producing revolvers all the time. He had got the impression that a revolver was as much a part of the ordinary, well-dressed man’s equipment in the United States as a collar.

===Chinese===
比尔沉吟起来。他向来不是脑筋转得快的人，这一问打得他措手不及。
“我想，是哪个家伙吧，”他终于眉开眼笑地说，“溜进来了，你知道的，手枪走了火。”
“可他带着手枪做什么？”
这话叫比尔有点犯迷糊。
“咦，他当然带着手枪啦，难道不是？我还以为这边人人都带着呢。”
除了本次短暂逗留期间亲眼所见的那些，达利什勋爵关于美国的知识，全然取材于他在伦敦看过的美国戏；戏里的老哥们儿随时随地都在掏左轮手枪。他由此得了一个印象：在美国，左轮手枪之于一个衣着体面的普通人，就如领子之于他，是行头里必备的一件。

===Original===
“I think it was a burglar,” said Elizabeth. “There have been a lot of burglaries down here this summer.”
“Would a burglar burgle the outhouse? Rummy idea, rather, what? Not much sense in it. I think it must have been a tramp. I expect tramps are always popping about and nosing into all sorts of extraordinary places, you know.”
“He must have been standing quite close to us while we were talking,” said Elizabeth with a shiver.
Bill looked about him. Everywhere was peace. No sinister sounds competed with the creaking of the tree frogs. No alien figures infested the landscape. The only alien figure, that of Mr. Pickering, was wedged into a bush, invisible to the naked eye.
“He’s gone now, at any rate,” he said. “What are we going to do?”

===Chinese===
“我看是个贼，”伊丽莎白说，“今年夏天这一带闹了好多起盗窃案。”
“贼会去偷棚屋？这想法可真够怪的，是不是？这里头没多少油水。我看准是个流浪汉。你知道的，流浪汉想必总是四处乱窜，专往各样古怪地方探头探脑。”
“咱们说话那会儿，他一定就站在离咱们很近的地方，”伊丽莎白说着打了个寒噤。
比尔朝四下里望了望。处处一片安宁。没有什么不祥的声响来同树蛙的格吱声争鸣。没有什么陌生身影出没于景物之间。唯一的陌生身影，也就是皮克林先生的那一尊，正卡在灌木丛里，肉眼无从得见。
“反正他这会儿走了，”他说，“咱们怎么办？”

===Original===
Elizabeth gave another shiver as she glanced hurriedly at the deceased. After life’s fitful fever Eustace slept well, but he was not looking his best.
“With﻿—it?” she said.
“I say,” advised Bill, “I shouldn’t call him ‘it,’ don’t you know. It sort of rubs it in. Why not ‘him’? I suppose we had better bury him. Have you a spade anywhere handy?”
“There isn’t a spade in the place.”
Bill looked thoughtful.

===Chinese===
伊丽莎白匆匆瞥了一眼死者，又打了个寒噤。一生的乖戾热病熬过之后，尤斯塔斯睡得正好，只是模样实在算不得他最体面的时候。
“拿——它？”她问。
“我说，”比尔进言道，“别管他叫‘它’，你知道的。这么叫有点往伤口上撒盐。叫‘他’不好吗？我看咱们顶好把他安葬了。手边哪儿有铁锹吗？”
“这地方压根没有铁锹。”
比尔露出沉思之色。

===Original===
“It takes weeks to make a hole with anything else, you know,” he said. “When I was a kid a friend of mine bet me I wouldn’t dig my way through to China with a pocket knife. It was an awful frost. I tried for a couple of days, and broke the knife and didn’t get anywhere near China.” He laid the remains on the grass and surveyed them meditatively. “This is what fellows always run up against in the detective novels﻿—what to do with the body. They manage the murder part of it all right, and then stub their toes on the body problem.”
“I wish you wouldn’t talk as if we had done a murder.”
“I feel as if we had, don’t you?”
“Exactly.”
“I read a story once where a fellow slugged somebody and melted the corpse down in a bathtub with sulphuric﻿—”

===Chinese===
“用别的家什挖坑，得挖上几个礼拜，你知道的，”他说，“小时候有个朋友跟我打赌，说我没法用一把小折刀一路挖到中国去。那回败得透心凉。我挖了两天，刀挖断了，离中国还差着十万八千里。”他把遗骸搁在草地上，若有所思地端详了一番。“侦探小说里的人物，总要撞上这一桩——尸首怎么处置。行凶的部分他们都料理得妥妥帖帖，末了却在尸体这道难题上绊了脚。”
“求你别说得好像咱们杀了人似的。”
“我感觉就像杀了人，你说是不是？”
“一点不错。”
“我读过一篇小说，里头一个家伙把人打倒，把尸首搁进浴缸用硫酸化——”

===Original===
“Stop! You’re making me sick!”
“Only a suggestion, don’t you know,” said Bill apologetically.
“Well, suggest something else then.”
“How about leaving him on Lady Wetherby’s doorstep? See what I mean﻿—let them take him in with the morning milk? Or, if you would rather, ring the bell and go away and﻿—you don’t think much of it?”
“I simply haven’t the nerve to do anything so risky.”
“Oh, I would do it. There would be no need for you to come.”

===Chinese===
“住口！你听得我要吐了！”
“不过是提个建议罢了，你知道的，”比尔道歉道。
“那就提点别的建议。”
“把他搁在韦瑟比夫人的门阶上怎么样？明白我的意思吗——让他们把他同清早的牛奶一道收进去？要不，要是你情愿，咱们按一按门铃就走开，然后——你嫌这主意不怎么样？”
“这么冒险的事，我实在没有那个胆子。”
“哦，我倒敢去。用不着你跟着。”

===Original===
“I wouldn’t dream of deserting you.”
“That’s awfully good of you.”
“Besides, I’m not going to be left alone tonight until I can jump into my little white bed and pull the clothes over my head. I’m scared. I’m just boneless with fright. And I wouldn’t go anywhere near Lady Wetherby’s doorstep with it.”
“Him.”
“It’s no use, I can’t think of it as him. It’s no good asking me to.”
Bill frowned thoughtfully.

===Chinese===
“我绝想不到要丢下你。”
“你真是太好了。”
“再说，今晚在跳上我的小白床、拉过被子蒙住脑袋之前，我可不要一个人孤零零待着。我怕。我吓得浑身骨头都酥了。而且我死也不肯拎着它上韦瑟比夫人的门阶跟前去。”
“是‘他’。”
“没用，我没法把它想成‘他’。求我也没用。”
比尔若有所思地皱起眉头。

===Original===
“I read a story once where two chappies wanted to get rid of a body. They put it inside a fellow’s piano.”
“You do seem to have read the most horrible sort of books.”
“I rather like a bit of blood with my fiction,” said Bill. “What about this piano scheme?”
“People only have talking machines in these parts.”
“I read a story﻿—”
“Let’s try to forget the stories you’ve read. Suggest something of your own.”

===Chinese===
“我读过一篇小说，两个家伙想打发掉一具尸首，就把它塞进了一个人的钢琴里。”
“你看的书，看来净是这类顶骇人的。”
“小说里带点儿血腥气，我倒挺喜欢，”比尔说，“钢琴这个主意怎么样？”
“这一带人家只有留声机。”
“我读过一篇小说——”
“咱们尽量忘了你读过的那些小说吧。你自己出个主意。”

===Original===
“Well, could we dissect the little chap?”
“Dissect him?”
“And bury him in the cellar, you know. Fellows do it to their wives.”
Elizabeth shuddered.
“Try again,” she said.
“Well, the only other thing I can think of is to take him into the woods and leave him there. It’s a pity we can’t let Lady Wetherby know where he is, she seems rather keen on him. But I suppose the main point is to get rid of him.”

===Chinese===
“呃，咱们能不能把这小家伙解剖了？”
“解剖他？”
“然后埋进地窖里，你知道的。有的家伙对自家太太就用这个法子。”
伊丽莎白毛骨悚然。
“再想一个，”她说。
“呃，我能想到的另外唯一的法子，就是把他弄进树林子，扔在那儿。可惜没法让韦瑟比夫人知道他落在哪儿，她好像还挺稀罕他。不过我想，要紧的是先把他打发掉。”

===Original===
“I know how we can do both. That’s a good idea of yours about the woods. They are part of Lady Wetherby’s property. I used to wander about there in the spring when the house was empty. There’s a sort of shack in the middle of them. I shouldn’t think anybody ever went there﻿—it’s a deserted sort of place. We could leave him there, and then﻿—well, we might write Lady Wetherby a letter or something. We could think out that part afterward.”
“It’s the best thing we’ve thought of. You really want to come?”
“If you attempt to leave here without me I shall scream. Let’s be starting.”
Bill picked Eustace up by his convenient tail.
“I read a story once,” he said, “where a fellow was lugging a corpse through a wood, when suddenly﻿—”
“Stop right there,” said Elizabeth firmly.

===Chinese===
“两件事我有一个一并办成的法子。你那个树林子的主意很好。树林是韦瑟比家产业的一部分。开春房子还空着的时候，我常在那儿闲逛。林子深处有一间窝棚模样的屋子。我想不会有什么人上那儿去——那是个荒废的所在。咱们可以把他留在那儿，然后——这个嘛，咱们可以给韦瑟比夫人写封信什么的。那一步往后再细想。”
“这是咱们想到的顶好的法子。你真要一起来？”
“你要是敢丢下我一个人走，我就尖叫。咱们动身吧。”
比尔捏住那条趁手的尾巴，把尤斯塔斯提了起来。
“我读过一篇小说，”他说，“一个家伙拖着死尸穿树林，忽然——”
“打住，就到这儿，”伊丽莎白斩钉截铁地说。

===Original===
During the conversation just recorded Dudley Pickering had been keeping a watchful eye on Bill and Elizabeth from the interior of a bush. His was not the ideal position for espionage, for he was too far off to hear what they said and the light was too dim to enable him to see what it was that Bill was holding. It looked to Mr. Pickering like a sack or bag of some sort. As time went by he became convinced that it was a sack, limp and empty at present but destined later to receive and bulge with what he believed was technically known as the swag. When the two objects of his vigilance concluded their lengthy consultation and moved off in the direction of Lady Wetherby’s woods, any doubts he may have had as to whether they were the criminals he had suspected them of being were dispersed. The whole thing worked out logically.
The Man, having spied out the land in his two visits to Lady Wetherby’s house, was now about to break in. His accomplice would stand by with the sack. With a beating heart Mr. Pickering gripped his revolver and moved round in the shadow of the shrubbery till he came to the gate, when he was just in time to see the guilty couple disappear into the woods. He followed them. He was glad to get on the move again. While he had been wedged into the bush quite a lot of the bush had been wedged into him. Something sharp had pressed against the calf of his leg and he had been pinched in a number of tender places. And he was convinced that one more of God’s unpleasant creatures had got down the back of his neck.
Dudley Pickering moved through the wood as snakily as he could. Nature had shaped him more for stability than for snakiness, but he did his best. He tingled with the excitement of the chase, and endeavored to creep through the undergrowth like one of those intelligent Indians of whom he had read so many years before in the pages of Mr. Fenimore Cooper. In those days Dudley Pickering had not thought very highly of Fenimore Cooper, holding his work deficient in serious and scientific interest; but now it seemed to him that there had been something in the man after all, and he resolved to get some of his books and go over them again. He wished he had read them more carefully at the time, for they doubtless contained much information and many hints which would have come in handy just now. He seemed, for example, to recall characters in them who had the knack of going through forests without letting a single twig crack beneath their feet. Probably the author had told you how this was done. In his unenlightened state it was beyond Mr. Pickering. The wood seemed carpeted with twigs. Whenever he stepped he trod on one and whenever he trod on one it cracked beneath his feet. There were moments when he felt gloomily that he might just as well be firing a machine gun.

===Chinese===
上面记下的这番对话进行之际，达德利·皮克林一直在一丛灌木的腹地里密切监视着比尔和伊丽莎白。这可不是干侦探勾当的理想位置：离得太远，听不见他们说什么；光又太暗，看不清比尔手里提的是什么物件。在皮克林先生眼里，那东西像一只口袋，或是诸如此类的包袋。时间一久，他认定那是一只口袋，眼下软塌塌空空如也，命中注定稍后便要装进——并且撑得鼓鼓囊囊——据他所知行话里叫作‘贼赃’的那路物件。等他监视的那两个目标结束了冗长的会商，朝韦瑟比夫人的树林方向移去，他心里但凡还残存的最后一丝疑虑——疑心他们究竟是不是他认定的那种罪犯——也烟消云散了。整件事顺理成章。
那个人先后两度探看韦瑟比夫人的宅子，把地势都踩好了，如今正要破门而入。他的同伙会拎着口袋在一旁接应。皮克林先生心头怦怦直跳，攥紧左轮手枪，借着树丛的暗影迂回挪到篱门，恰赶上望见那对罪人消失在树林里。他跟了上去。能重新活动筋骨，他大为快慰。方才卡在灌木丛里的那段时候，也有好大一片灌木卡进了他身上。一样尖利的东西硌着他的小腿肚，好几处嫩肉都遭了拧掐。他并且确信，上帝造的讨嫌造物里，又有一个钻进了他的后脖领。
达德利·皮克林竭尽所能，蛇行狸步地在林中挪动。造化当初捏他的时候，图的是稳重，不是灵活，可他尽了力。追猎的兴奋叫他浑身发麻，他卖力地想学多年前从费尼莫尔·库珀（Fenimore Cooper）先生书页里读到的那些机灵的印第安人，在矮树丛中匍匐潜行。当年，达德利·皮克林对费尼莫尔·库珀评价不高，认为他的作品缺乏严肃的科学趣味；如今在他看来，此人笔下到底还是有几分门道的，他决计弄几本来重新温习一遍。他只恨当年读得不够仔细，那些书里无疑藏着大量知识和种种提示，此刻正用得上。譬如他仿佛记得，书里有些人物有一手绝活：穿过森林，脚底下竟不踩断一根细枝。这本事怎么练成，作者多半交代过。以皮克林先生眼下的蒙昧程度，实在无从领会。这片林子好像满地铺着细枝。他一迈脚就踩上一根，一踩上，那根就在他脚底下咔嚓作响。有几次他灰心地觉得，自己简直不如架起一挺机枪扫射算了。

===Original===
Bill, meanwhile, Elizabeth following close behind him, was plowing his way onward. From time to time he would turn to administer some encouraging remark, for it had come home to him by now that encouraging remarks were what she needed very much in the present crisis of her affairs. She was showing him a new and hitherto unsuspected side of her character. The Elizabeth whom he had known﻿—the valiant, self-reliant Elizabeth﻿—had gone, leaving in her stead someone softer, more appealing, more approachable. It was this that was filling him with strange emotions as he led the way to their destination.
He was becoming more and more conscious of a sense of being drawn very near to Elizabeth, of a desire to soothe, comfort and protect her. It was as if tonight he had discovered the missing key to a puzzle or the missing element in some chemical combination. Like most big men, his mind was essentially a protective mind; weakness drew out the best that was in him. And it was only tonight that Elizabeth had given any sign of having any weakness in her composition. That clear vision which had come to him on his long walk came again now, that vivid conviction that she was the only girl in the world for him.
He was debating within himself the advisability of trying to find words to express this sentiment, when Mr. Pickering, the modern Chingachgook, trod on another twig in the background and Elizabeth stopped abruptly with a little cry.

===Chinese===
比尔这边呢，伊丽莎白紧紧跟在身后，正深一脚浅一脚地往前趟。他不时回过头去，颁上几句打气的话，因为到了这会儿他已深深体会：在她事情的眼前这场危局里，顶用得着的正是打气的话。她正把性格中新的一面显露给他，那是一向无人料到的一面。他所认识的那个伊丽莎白——那个英气勃勃、凡事自立的伊丽莎白——不见了，接替她的是个更柔软、更惹人怜、更可亲近的人。正是这个，在他领路奔赴目的地的当儿，叫他心里涨满了奇异的情绪。
他越来越清晰地意识到，自己同伊丽莎白贴得这样近，心里涌动着抚慰她、体贴她、保护她的渴望。仿佛就在今夜，他找到了一把谜题上缺失的钥匙，或是某种化合物里缺的那样元素。同多数大个子一样，他的心性骨子里是一副保护者的心性；柔弱最能把他身上最好的东西引出来。而直到今夜，伊丽莎白才头一回露出她性情里也有柔弱的一角。那晚长途跋涉中降临的澄明眼光，此刻又回来了——那个鲜活的信念：天下的姑娘，唯有她与他相配。
他正在心里权衡，要不要设法找几句话把这份情意说出来，忽听得后面的皮克林先生——当代的金加古（Chingachgook）——又踩响了一根细枝，伊丽莎白猛地站住，低低惊叫了一声。

===Original===
“What was that?” she demanded breathlessly.
Bill had heard a noise too. It was impossible to be within a dozen yards of Mr. Pickering, when on the trail, and not hear a noise. The suspicion that someone was following them did not come to him, for he was a man rather of common sense than of imagination, and common sense was asking him bluntly why the deuce anybody should want to tramp after them through a wood at that time of night. He caught the note of panic in Elizabeth’s voice, and was soothing her.
“It was just a branch breaking. You hear all sorts of rum noises in a wood.”
“I believe it’s the man with the pistol following us!”
“Nonsense. Why should he? Silly thing to do!”
He spoke almost severely.

===Chinese===
“那是什么？”她上气不接下气地追问。
比尔也听见了响动。只要在皮克林先生十二码之内，又赶上他正循迹潜行，想不听见响动都不可能。有人跟踪的疑头并没往他心里去，因为他这个人，长的是常识，缺的是想象力；而常识正直截了当地质问他：深更半夜，见鬼的会有谁乐意踩着他们穿一片林子？他捕捉到伊丽莎白嗓音里的慌乱，便温言安抚她。
“不过是树枝断了。树林里各样怪声音都听得见。”
“我看是那个带手枪的人在跟咱们！”
“胡说。他跟咱们做什么？干那种蠢事！”
他说得几乎有些严厉。

===Original===
“Look!” cried Elizabeth.
“What?”
“I saw someone dodge behind that tree.”
“You mustn’t let yourself imagine things. Buck up!”
“I can’t buck up. I’m scared.”
“Pull yourself together.”

===Chinese===
“瞧！”伊丽莎白嚷道。
“瞧什么？”
“我看见有人一闪身躲到那棵树后面去了。”
“你可不许疑神疑鬼。打起精神来！”
“我打不起精神。我怕。”
“镇定一点。”

===Original===
“I can’t.”
“Which tree did you think you saw someone dodge behind?”
“That big one there.”
“Well, listen. I’ll go back and﻿—”
“If you leave me for an instant I shall die in agonies.” She gulped. “I never knew I was such an awful coward before. I’m just a worm.”

===Chinese===
“镇定不了。”
“你觉得看见有人躲到哪棵树后面去了？”
“那边那棵大的。”
“那好，听着。我回去瞧瞧，然后——”
“你敢离开我一眨眼的工夫，我就得活活吓死。”她咽了口唾沫。“我从前竟不知道自己是这么个十足的胆小鬼。我就是一条虫。”

===Original===
“Nonsense. This sort of thing might frighten anyone. I read a story once﻿—”
“Don’t!”
Bill found that his heart had suddenly begun to beat with unaccustomed rapidity. The desire to soothe, comfort and protect Elizabeth became the immediate ambition of his life. It was very dark where they stood. The moonlight which fell in little patches round them did not penetrate the thicket which they had entered. He could hardly see her. He was merely aware of her as a presence, an appealing and feminine presence.

===Chinese===
“胡说。这种事搁谁身上都要吓着。我读过一篇小说——”
“别说了！”
比尔发觉自己的心突然怦怦疾跳起来，跳得不合常轨。抚慰、体贴、保护伊丽莎白，霎时成了他此生最迫切的心愿。他们站的地方极黑。月光只在他们四周落下斑斑驳驳的几点，照不进他们走进的这片密丛。他几乎看不见她。他只是意识到她人在跟前——一个惹人怜惜的、属于女性的存在。

===Original===
An excellent idea occurred to him.
“Hold my hand,” he said.
It was what he would have said to a frightened child, and there was much of the frightened child about Elizabeth then. The Eustace mystery had given her a shock which subsequent events had done nothing to dispel, and she had lost that jauntiness and self-confidence which was her natural armor against the more ordinary happenings of life.
Something small and soft slid gratefully into his palm, and there was silence for a space. Bill said nothing. Elizabeth said nothing. And Mr. Pickering had stopped treading on twigs. The faintest of night breezes ruffled the treetops above them. The moonbeams filtered through the branches. He held her hand tightly.

===Chinese===
一个绝妙的主意浮上他的心头。
“牵着我的手。”他说。
这话原是对受了惊的孩子说的，而此刻的伊丽莎白，身上也确有几分受惊的孩子气。尤斯塔斯之谜给她的那场惊吓，后续种种变故丝毫无济于驱散；她那份活泼泼的自信——她对付日常琐事的天生铠甲——已经卸了下来。
一个小小的、软软的东西带着感激滑进他的掌心，一时四下无声。比尔没说话。伊丽莎白没说话。皮克林先生也不再踩响细枝了。极轻极轻的夜风拂动着他们头顶的树梢。月光从枝叶间筛下来。他把她的手攥得紧紧的。

===Original===
“Better?”
“Much.”
The breeze died away. Not a leaf stirred. The wood was very still. Somewhere on a bough a bird moved drowsily.
“All right?”
“Yes.”
And then something happened, something shattering, disintegrating. It was only a pheasant, but it sounded like the end of the world. It rose at their feet with a rattle that filled the universe, and for a moment all was black confusion. And when that moment had passed it became apparent to Bill that his arm was round Elizabeth, that she was sobbing helplessly and that he was kissing her. Somebody was talking very rapidly in a low voice. The fellow seemed to be babbling.

===Chinese===
“好些了？”
“好多了。”
风歇了。一片叶子也不摇动。林中静极了。不知哪根枝子上，一只鸟睡意昏昏地挪动了一下。
“没事了？”
“嗯。”
接着出了一桩事，一桩天崩地裂、魂飞魄散的事。那不过是一只雉鸡，可听起来像世界末日。它就在他们脚边轰然腾起，一阵扑棱声灌满了天地宇宙，一时间万物没入漆黑的混乱。等那一刻过去，比尔才发觉自己已经搂住了伊丽莎白，她已经无助地啜泣起来，而他正在吻她。有人在低声急促地说话。那家伙简直语无伦次。

===Original===
He found that it was himself.
“Elizabeth!”
There was something wonderful about the name, a sort of music. This was odd, because the name, as a name, was far from being a favorite of his. Until that moment childish associations had prejudiced him against it. It had been inextricably involved in his mind with an atmosphere of stuffy schoolrooms and general misery, for it had been his misfortune that his budding mind was constitutionally incapable of remembering who had been queen of England at the time of the Spanish Armada﻿—a fact that had caused a good deal of friction with a rather sharp-tempered governess. But now it seemed the only possible name for a girl to have, the only label that could even remotely suggest those feminine charms which he found in this girl beside him. There was poetry in every syllable of it. It was like one of those deep chords which fill the hearer with vague yearnings for strange and beautiful things. He asked for nothing better than to stand here repeating it again and again.
“Elizabeth!”
“Bill, dear!”
That sounded good too. There was music in “Bill” when properly spoken. The reason why all the other Bills in the world had got the impression that it was a prosaic sort of name was that there was only one girl in existence capable of speaking it properly, and she was not for them.

===Chinese===
他发觉说话的正是自己。
“伊丽莎白！”
这名字里有种奇妙的东西，一种音乐似的魅力。说来也怪，这名字单论名字，从来不合他的口味。直到那一刻，孩提时代的联想一直叫他对它心存芥蒂。在他脑子里，它同闷热的教室和一概的苦日子纠缠得难解难分，因为他命途多舛：他那颗初萌的头脑天生记不住西班牙无敌舰队（Spanish Armada）年代英格兰的女王是谁——这桩事实惹得一位脾气相当尖刻的女家庭教师同他摩擦不断。可如今，它仿佛成了姑娘唯一配得上的名字，唯一的标签，唯有它还勉强能暗示他在这身旁姑娘身上发现的那些女性魅力。它的每一个音节里都有诗。它像一根低沉的和弦，听得人心里泛起对奇异而美好事物的悠悠渴慕。他别无他求，只想站在这儿，一遍又一遍地念它。
“伊丽莎白！”
“比尔，亲爱的！”
这一听也美妙。“比尔”两个字，只要念得得法，自有它的音乐。世上其余那些比尔之所以觉得这是个平淡无味的名字，是因为普天之下只有一个姑娘念得得法，而她不属于他们。

===Original===
“Bill, are you really fond of me?”
“Fond of you!”
Elizabeth gave a little sigh.
“You’re so splendid!”
Bill was staggered. These were strange words. He had never thought much of himself. He had always looked on himself as rather a chump﻿—well-meaning, perhaps, but an awful ass. It seemed incredible that anyone﻿—and Elizabeth of all people﻿—could look on him as splendid.

===Chinese===
“比尔，你真心喜欢我吗？”
“喜欢你！”
伊丽莎白轻轻叹了口气。
“你这样了不起！”
比尔愣住了。这几句话可真新鲜。他从来没把自己看得多重。他一向把自己看作一个相当愚蠢的糊涂虫——心眼兴许不坏，可十足是一头蠢驴。居然有人——偏偏是伊丽莎白——会把他看作一个了不起的人物，简直难以置信。

===Original===
And yet the very fact that she had said it gave it a plausible sort of sound. It shook his convictions. Splendid! Was he? By Jove, perhaps he was, what? Rum idea, but it grew on a chap. Filled with a novel feeling of exaltation, he kissed Elizabeth eleven times in rapid succession.
He felt devilish fit. He would have liked to run a mile or two and jump a few gates. He wished five or six starving beggars would come along; it would be pleasant to give the poor blighters money. It was too much to expect at that time of night, of course, but it would be rather jolly if Jess Willard or somebody would roll up and try to pick a quarrel. He would show them something. He felt grand and strong and full of beans. What a ripping thing life was when you came to think of it!
“This,” he said, “is perfectly extraordinary!”
And time stood still.
A sense of something incongruous jarred upon Bill. Something seemed to be interfering with the supreme romance of that golden moment. It baffled him at first. Then he realized that he was still holding Eustace by the tail.
Dudley Pickering had watched these proceedings as well as the fact that it was extremely dark and that he was endeavoring to hide a portly form behind a slender bush would permit him﻿—with a sense of bewilderment. A comic artist, drawing Mr. Pickering at that moment, would no doubt have placed above his head one of those large marks of interrogation which lend vigor and snap to modern comic art. Certainly such a mark of interrogation would have summed up his feelings exactly. Of what was taking place he had not the remotest notion. All he knew was that for some inexplicable reason his quarry had come to a halt and seemed to have settled down for an indefinite stay. Voices came to him in an indistinguishable murmur, intensely irritating to a conscientious tracker. One of Fenimore Cooper’s Indians﻿—notably Chingachgook, if, which seemed incredible, that was really the man’s name﻿—would have crept up without a sound and heard what was being said and got in on the ground floor of whatever plot was being hatched. But experience had taught Mr. Pickering that, superior as he was to Chingachgook and his friends in many ways, as a creeper he was not in their class. He weighed thirty or forty pounds more than a first-class creeper should. Besides, creeping is like golf. You can’t take it up in the middle forties and expect to compete with those who have been at it from infancy.

===Chinese===
然而话既出于她之口，这评语便自然添了几分可信的成色。他的固有之见动摇了。了不起！他是吗？说老实话，说不定还真是，是不是？这念头虽然古怪，却在一个家伙心里越盘越牢。他满怀一种从未体味过的陶然得意，一口气连吻了伊丽莎白十一遍。
他只觉得浑身痛快，精神抖擞得邪乎。他真想撒腿跑上一两英里，再蹦过几道栅门。他巴不得迎面来上五六个挨饿的叫花子，好给这几个可怜的家伙散散钱——必定其乐无穷。当然，深更半夜指望这个未免非分，可要是杰斯·威拉德（Jess Willard）之流滚上门来寻衅，倒也妙不可言。他要让他们瞧瞧厉害。他觉得自己威风八面、孔武有力、精神百倍。细想起来，人生这东西可真美得很！
“这，”他说，“真是奇妙无比！”
时间凝然不动。
一种不谐之感戳痛了比尔。似乎有什么东西横插进来，搅扰着那黄金时刻的无上罗曼司。起初他摸不着头脑。随后他醒悟过来：自己那只手里还拎着尤斯塔斯的尾巴。
达德利·皮克林把眼前这一幕尽目力所能及看在眼里——所谓目力所能及，是以天色漆黑为限，外加他正竭力把一具臃肿的身躯藏到一丛细瘦的灌木背后——心中一片茫然。此刻若有位滑稽画家为皮克林先生造像，无疑会在他头顶安上一个特大号的问号——正是这类大问号给现代滑稽艺术平添生猛与活泼。毫无疑问，这样一个问号恰可概括他此刻的心境。眼下的种种，他连一丝一毫的概念也没有。他只知道：由于某个无法解释的缘故，他追踪的猎物停了下来，而且看样子打定主意要就地驻扎，驻期不设上限。人声一阵阵传进他耳朵，只是些分辨不出的絮语，这对一个恪尽职守的追踪者是莫大的熬煎。换了费尼莫尔·库珀笔下的印第安人——尤其是金加古，倘若那当真是此人的名字，叫人难以置信——早已悄无声息地潜行近前，听清他们在说些什么，在正在酝酿的任何阴谋里从打地基时就入了伙。然而经验早已教训过皮克林先生：他虽然在许多方面胜过金加古和他的朋友们，论潜行匍匐的功夫，却同他们不是一个档次。比起一位一流的爬行者应有的体重，他足足超出了三四十磅。况且，潜行这门功夫同高尔夫是一个道理。人到了四十五岁上下才入门，休想同打从襁褓里就练起的人一较短长。

===Original===
He had resigned himself to an all-night vigil behind the bush, when to his great delight he perceived that things had begun to move again. There was a rustling of feet in the undergrowth, and he could just see two indistinct forms making their way among the bushes. He came out of his hiding place and followed stealthily, or as stealthily as the fact that he had not even taken a correspondence course in creeping allowed. And profiting by earlier mistakes, he did succeed in making far less noise than before. In place of his former somewhat elephantine method of progression he adopted a species of shuffle which had excellent results, for it enabled him to brush twigs away instead of stepping flat-footedly on them. The new method was slow, but it had no other disadvantages.
Because it was slow Mr. Pickering was obliged to follow his prey almost entirely by ear. It was easy at first, for they seemed to be hurrying on regardless of noise. Then unexpectedly the sounds of their passage ceased.
He halted. In his boyish way the first thing he thought was that it was an ambush. He had a vision of that large man suspecting his presence and lying in wait for him with a revolver. This was not a comforting thought. Of course, if a man is going to fire a revolver at you it makes little difference whether he is a giant or a pygmy, but Mr. Pickering was in no frame of mind for nice reasoning. It was the thought of Bill’s physique which kept him standing there irresolute.
What would Chingachgook﻿—assuming, for purposes of argument, that any sane godfather could really have given a helpless child a name like that﻿—have done? He would, Mr. Pickering considered, after giving the matter his earnest attention, have made a detour and outflanked the enemy. An excellent solution of the difficulty. Mr. Pickering turned to the left and began to advance circuitously, with the result that, before he knew what he was doing, he came out into a clearing and understood the meaning of the sudden silence which had perplexed him. Footsteps made no sound on this mossy turf.

===Chinese===
他已打算死心塌地在灌木背后守上一整夜，忽然喜出望外地发现，事情又动起来了。矮树丛里响起窸窸窣窣的脚步，他依稀望见两个模糊的影子在灌木间穿行。他从藏身之处出来，蹑足跟上——所谓蹑足，是以他连潜行的函授课程都不曾报读过为限度的。他吸取了先前失手的教训，果然闹出的动静比先前小得多。他舍了原先那套略嫌大象踩泥的行进法，改用一种蹭步，效果极佳：蹭步能把细枝拂到一旁，不像从前那样一脚踏个正着。新法子慢是慢，此外别无缺点。
正因为慢，皮克林先生只好几乎全凭耳朵追踪他的猎物。起初倒不难，他们似乎只顾赶路，顾不得声响。随后出其不意，那行路声戛然而止。
他站住了。以他的孩子气，头一个念头便是：这是埋伏。他眼前立时浮现那大个子起疑设伏、举着左轮等他自投罗网的图景。这念头可叫人宽慰不了。诚然，一个人若要朝你开枪，他是巨人还是侏儒原没多大分别；可皮克林先生此刻的心情，已不宜作精细的推理了。叫他六神无主地僵立原地的，正是比尔那副体格所引起的念头。
换了金加古——姑且为立论方便，假定天下真有哪个头脑清醒的教父，当真肯给一个无助的婴孩起这样一个名字——会怎么办？皮克林先生认真思量了一番，认定他会绕道迂回，抄敌人的侧翼。好一个解决难题的妙策。皮克林先生转身向左，兜起圈子来，结果还没弄清自己在干什么，已经走进了一片林中空地，顿时明白了方才那阵突如其来的沉寂是怎么回事。这片覆着青苔的草地，脚步落在上面悄然无声。

===Original===
He knew where he was now, the clearing was familiar. This was where Lord Wetherby’s shack studio stood; and there it was, right in front of him, black and clear in the moonlight. And the two dark figures were going into it.
Mr. Pickering retreated into the shelter of the bushes and mused upon this thing. It seemed to him that for centuries he had been doing nothing but retreat into bushes for this purpose. His perplexity had returned. He could imagine no reason why burglars should want to visit Lord Wetherby’s studio. He had taken it for granted, when he had tracked them to the clearing, that they were on their way to the house, which was quite close to the shack, separated from it only by a thin belt of trees and a lawn.
They had certainly gone in. He had seen them with his own eyes﻿—first The Man, then very close behind him, apparently holding to his coat, the girl. But why?
Creep up and watch them? Would Chingachgook have taken a risk like that? Hardly, unless insured with some good company. Then what? He was still undecided when he perceived the objects of his attention emerging. He backed a little farther into the bushes.
They stood for an instant, listening apparently. The Man no longer carried the sack. They exchanged a few inaudible words. Then they crossed the clearing and entered the wood a few yards to his right. He could hear the crackling of their footsteps diminishing in the direction of the road.
A devouring curiosity seized upon Mr. Pickering. He wanted, more than he had wanted almost anything before in his life, to find out what the dickens they had been up to in there. He listened. The footsteps were no longer audible. He ran across the clearing and into the shack. It was then that he discovered that he had no matches.

===Chinese===
他如今认出这是哪儿了，这片空地眼熟。韦瑟比勋爵那间窝棚画室就立在这里；那儿，就在他正前方，月光下黑黢黢清清楚楚。那两条黑影正朝里走。
皮克林先生退回灌木丛的掩蔽里，冥想这桩事。在他感觉里，仿佛千百年来他一直在干的就是这一件事：为了这个缘故退进灌木丛。他的困惑又回来了。窃贼巴巴地光临韦瑟比勋爵的画室，图个什么，他想不出。方才一路把他们追到空地时，他本来认定他们是要奔那幢房子去的；房子离窝棚很近，中间只隔着一溜疏树和一片草坪。
他们确乎进去了。他亲眼所见——头里是那个人，紧跟着，显然拽着他的外衣，是那姑娘。可是为什么？
潜上去盯着他们？换了金加古肯冒这个险吗？很难说，除非他在哪家像样的保险公司保过险。那么怎么办？他还没拿定主意，就见他注目的那两个目标出来了。他往灌木丛深处又退了几步。
两人站了片刻，似乎在侧耳细听。那个人手里不再拎着口袋。他们交谈了几句，听不见字句。随后他们穿过空地，进了他右侧几码开外的树林。他听见他们咔嚓咔嚓的脚步声朝大路方向渐去渐远。
一股吞噬人的好奇心攫住了皮克林先生。他这辈子几乎没有什么时候比这会儿更想弄清：他们到底在那里面搞了些什么鬼名堂。他侧耳细听。脚步声已不可闻。他奔过空地，一头钻进窝棚。也正是这时，他发现自己身上没有火柴。

===Original===
This needless infliction, coming upon him at the crisis of an adventurous night, infuriated Mr. Pickering. He swore softly. He groped round the walls for an electric-light switch, but the shack had no electric-light switches. When there was need to illuminate it an oil lamp performed the duty. This occurred to Mr. Pickering after he had been round the place three times, and he ceased to grope for switches and began to seek a matchbox. He was still seeking it when he was frozen in his tracks by the sound of footsteps, muffled but by their nearness audible, just outside the door. He pulled out his pistol, which he had replaced in his pocket, backed against the wall, and stood there, prepared to sell his life dearly. The door opened.
One reads of desperate experiences aging people in a single night. His present predicament aged Mr. Pickering in a single minute. In the brief interval of time between the opening of the door and the moment when a voice outside began to speak, he became a full thirty years older. His boyish ardor slipped from him, and he was once more the Dudley Pickering whom the world knew, the staid and respectable middle-aged man of affairs, who would have given a million dollars not to have got himself mixed up in this deplorable business.
And then the voice spoke.

===Chinese===
这场全然多余的无妄之灾，偏在他冒险之夜的紧要关头临头，把皮克林先生气坏了。他低声咒骂。他沿墙摸索电灯开关，可窝棚里根本没有电灯开关。需要照明的时候，自有一盏油灯当值。皮克林先生绕着屋子转了三圈，才想起这一层，于是不再摸开关，改找火柴盒。火柴盒还没找着，门外突起的脚步声把他钉在了原地——那声音发闷，却近在咫尺，听得真切。他掏出方才揣回兜里的手枪，背抵着墙，站在那儿，决意豁出性命也要拼个够本。门开了。
书上读到过，叫人绝望的经历能在一夜之间使人变老。他眼下的处境，却在一分钟之内就叫皮克林先生变老了。从门开那一刻，到门外有人开口说话，这短短一段工夫里，他足足老了三十岁。少年人的热血从他身上滑落，他重又是世人熟知的那个达德利·皮克林，那个稳重体面的中年实业家——那个宁可掏出一百万美元、只求自己不曾卷进这桩可悲勾当的人。
而后，那声音开口了。

===Original===
“I’ll light the lamp,” it said; and with an overpowering feeling of relief Mr. Pickering recognized it as Lord Wetherby’s. A moment later the temperamental peer’s dapper figure became visible in silhouette against a background of pale light.
“Ah-hum!” said Mr. Pickering.
The effect on Lord Wetherby was remarkable. To hear someone clear his throat at the back of a dark room, where there should rightfully be no throat to be cleared, would cause even your man of stolid habit a passing thrill. The thing got right in among Lord Wetherby’s highly sensitive ganglions like an earthquake. He leaped in the air with a strangled cry, then dashed out and slammed the door behind him.
“There’s someone in there!”
Lady Wetherby’s tranquil voice made itself heard.
“Nonsense, who could be in there?”

===Chinese===
“我来点灯。”那声音说；皮克林先生如释重负，听出那是韦瑟比勋爵。片刻之后，微光衬出的剪影里，显出了那位神经质的勋爵清瘦利落的身形。
“啊——咳！”皮克林先生说。
这一嗓子在韦瑟比勋爵身上收效之巨，蔚为奇观。在一间黑屋子的深处听见有人清嗓子——那屋里论理根本不该有可供清点的嗓子——便连那号木头木脑的人物也要惊魂一瞬。这一下径直打进韦瑟比勋爵那些高度敏感的神经节里，如地震一般。他一声噎住的怪叫蹦上半空，随即冲出门去，砰地带上了门。
“屋里有人！”
传来了韦瑟比夫人那安稳平静的嗓音。
“胡说，屋里能有什么人？”

===Original===
“I heard him, I tell you. He growled at me!”
It seemed to Mr. Pickering that the time had come to relieve the mental distress which he was causing his host. He raised his voice.
“It’s all right!” he called.
“There!” said Lord Wetherby.
“Who’s that?” asked Lady Wetherby through the door.
“It’s all right. It’s me﻿—Pickering.”

===Chinese===
“我听见他了，我告诉你。他还冲我嗥了一嗓子！”
皮克林先生觉得，该到了解除自己给主人造成的精神痛苦的时候了。他提高嗓门。
“没事！”他喊道。
“听见没有！”韦瑟比勋爵说。
“是谁？”韦瑟比夫人隔着门问。
“没事。是我——皮克林。”

===Original===
The door was opened a few inches by a cautious hand.
“Is that you, Pickering?”
“Yes. It’s all right.”
“Don’t keep saying it’s all right,” said Lord Wetherby irritably. “It isn’t all right. What do you mean by hiding in the dark and popping out and barking at a man? You made me bite my tongue. I’ve never had such a shock in my life.”
Mr. Pickering left his lair and came out into the open. Lord Wetherby was looking aggrieved, Lady Wetherby peacefully inquisitive. For the first time Mr. Pickering discovered that Claire was present. She was standing behind Lady Wetherby with a floating white something over her head, looking very beautiful.
“For the love of Mike!” said Lady Wetherby.

===Chinese===
一只小心翼翼的手把门拉开了一条几英寸宽的缝。
“是你吗，皮克林？”
“是我。没事。”
“别一口一个没事了，”韦瑟比勋爵气咻咻地说，“根本有事。你躲在人家的黑地里，冷不防蹦出来，冲着人乱吼，你这是什么意思？害我把舌头都咬破了。活这么大，我没受过这样的惊吓。”
皮克林先生走出他的巢穴，来到亮处。韦瑟比勋爵一脸委屈，韦瑟比夫人一派安详的好奇。皮克林先生这才头一回发现克莱尔也在场。她站在韦瑟比夫人身后，头上罩着一件轻飘飘的白色物事，美极了。
“我的老天爷！”韦瑟比夫人说。

===Original===
Mr. Pickering became aware that he was still holding the revolver.
“Oh, ah!” he said, and trousered the weapon.
“Barking at people!” muttered Lord Wetherby in a querulous undertone.
“What on earth are you doing, Dudley?” said Claire.
There was a note in her voice which both puzzled and pained Mr. Pickering, a note that seemed to suggest that she found herself in imperfect sympathy with him. Her expression deepened the suggestion. It was a cold expression, unfriendly, as if it was not so keen a pleasure to Claire to look at him as it should be for a girl to look at the man whom she is engaged to marry. He had noticed the same note in her voice and the same hostile look in her eye earlier in the evening. He had found her alone, reading a letter, which, as the stamp on the envelope showed, had come from England. She had seemed so upset that he had asked her if it contained bad news, and she had replied in the negative with so much irritation that he had desisted from inquiries. But his own idea was that she had had bad news from home. Mr. Pickering still clung to his early impression that her little brother Percy was consumptive, and he thought the child must have taken a turn for the worse. It was odd that she should have looked and spoken like that, and it was odd that she should look and speak like that now. He had been vaguely disturbed then and he was vaguely disturbed now. He had the feeling that all was not well.

===Chinese===
皮克林先生这才留意到，自己手里还端着那把左轮。
“哦，嗬！”他说，把家伙揣进了裤兜。
“冲着人乱吼！”韦瑟比勋爵怨气冲天，低声咕哝。
“你究竟在这儿干什么呢，达德利？”克莱尔说。
她嗓音里有种调子，叫皮克林先生又困惑又心痛，那调子仿佛在表示：她觉得自己同他并不怎么同心同德。她的神色更把这一层坐实了。那是一副冷淡的神色，不友善，仿佛克莱尔看着他不觉得有多欢喜，而一个姑娘看着自己订了婚将要嫁的人，本该满心欢喜才是。今晚早些时候，他就留意过她嗓音里同样的调子、她眼里同样的敌意。他撞见她独自一人读一封信，从信封上的邮票看，信来自英国。她心绪坏得厉害，他便问信里是不是有什么噩耗；她没好气地一口否认，他也就不再追问。可他私下认定，她是家里来了噩耗。皮克林先生至今咬定早先的印象：她的小弟弟珀西害着痨病，他想这孩子准是病势转沉了。她方才那副神色、那种口吻，古怪；她此刻这副神色、这种口吻，也古怪。当时他就隐约不安，此刻又隐约不安。他总觉得，什么地方不对劲。

===Original===
“Yes,” said Lady Wetherby. “What on earth are you doing, Dudley?”
“Popping out!” grumbled Lord Wetherby.
“We came here to see Algie’s picture, which has got something wrong with its eyes apparently, and we find you hiding in the dark with a gun. What’s the idea?”
“It’s a long story,” said Mr. Pickering.
“We have the night before us,” said Lady Wetherby. “Push it out.”

===Chinese===
“就是，”韦瑟比夫人说，“你究竟在这儿干什么呢，达德利？”
“冷不防往外蹦！”韦瑟比勋爵咕哝道。
“我们过来是要看阿尔吉那张画的——那张画的眼睛显然有毛病——倒撞见你揣着枪躲在黑地里。这是唱的哪一出？”
“说来话长，”皮克林先生说。
“夜还长着呢，”韦瑟比夫人说，“往外倒吧。”

===Original===
“You remember The Man﻿—the fellow I found looking in at the window﻿—The Man who said he knew Claire?”
“You’ve got that man on the brain, Dudley. What’s he been doing to you now?”
“I tracked him here.”
“Tracked him? Where from?”
“From that bee farm place where he’s living. He and that girl you spoke of went into these woods. I thought they were making for the house, but they went into the shack.”
“Studio,” said Lord Wetherby.

===Chinese===
“你还记得那个人吗——我撞见他扒着窗子往里张望的那一位——那个自称认识克莱尔的人？”
“你满脑子都是那个人了，达德利。这回他又把你怎么了？”
“我一路追踪他到了这儿。”
“追踪他？从哪儿跟来的？”
“从他住的那个养蜂场。他同你说起的那个姑娘进了这片树林。我当他们要奔房子去，他们却钻进了窝棚。”
“画室。”韦瑟比勋爵说。

===Original===
“Studio,” said Mr. Pickering.
“What did they do then?” asked Lady Wetherby.
“They came out again.”
“Why?”
“That’s what I was trying to find out.”
Lord Wetherby uttered an exclamation.

===Chinese===
“画室。”皮克林先生说。
“后来他们干了什么？”韦瑟比夫人问。
“又出来了。”
“为什么？”
“我要弄清的正是这个。”
韦瑟比勋爵惊呼了一声。

===Original===
“By Jove!” There was apprehension in his voice, but mingled with it a certain pleased surprise. “Perhaps they were after my picture. I’ll light the lamp. Good Lord, picture thieves﻿—Romneys﻿—missing Gainsboroughs﻿—” His voice trailed off as he found the lamp and lit it. Relief and disappointment were nicely blended in his next words. “No, it’s still there.”
The soft light of the lamp filled the studio.
“Well, that’s a comfort,” said Lady Wetherby, sauntering in. “We couldn’t afford to lose﻿—Oh!”
Lord Wetherby spun round as her scream burst upon his already tortured nerve centers. Lady Wetherby was kneeling on the floor. Claire hurried in.
“What is it, Polly?”

===Chinese===
“天哪！”他嗓音里带着惊惶，却又掺着几分欣然的意外。“说不定他们是冲我那张画来的。我来点灯。老天爷，画贼——罗姆尼（Romney）——失窃的庚斯博罗（Gainsborough）——”他寻着灯点上，声音渐渐低下去。宽慰与失望在他接下来说的话里调配得恰到好处。“不，画还在。”
柔和的灯光漫满了画室。
“唉，这就放心了，”韦瑟比夫人信步踱进来，“咱们可丢不起——呀！”
那声尖叫劈头砸向他本已备受折磨的神经中枢，韦瑟比勋爵猛地转过身来。韦瑟比夫人跪在地上。克莱尔急忙进来。
“怎么啦，波莉？”

===Original===
Lady Wetherby rose to her feet and pointed. Her face had lost its look of patient amusement. It was hard and set. She eyed Mr. Pickering in a menacing way.
“Look!”
Claire followed her finger.
“Good gracious! It’s Eustace!”
“Shot?”
She was looking intently at Mr. Pickering.

===Chinese===
韦瑟比夫人立起身来，伸手指着。她脸上那副耐着性子的笑意荡然无存，换上了一副冷硬僵直的面孔。她逼视着皮克林先生，眼色咄咄逼人。
“看！”
克莱尔顺着她的手指望去。
“我的天哪！是尤斯塔斯！”
“打死的？”
她目不转睛地盯住皮克林先生。

===Original===
“Well, Dudley,” she said coldly, “what about it?”
Mr. Pickering found that they were all looking at him﻿—Lady Wetherby with glittering eyes, Claire with cool scorn, Lord Wetherby with a horror which he seemed to have achieved with something of an effort.
“Well!” said Claire.
“What about it, Dudley?” said Lady Wetherby.
“I must say, Pickering,” said Lord Wetherby, “much as I disliked the animal, it’s a bit thick!”
Mr. Pickering recoiled from their accusing gaze.

===Chinese===
“好啊，达德利，”她冷冷地说，“这怎么说？”
皮克林先生发觉，他们全都望着他——韦瑟比夫人两眼放光，克莱尔一脸冷峭的轻蔑，韦瑟比勋爵满脸惊骇，那副惊骇像是费了点劲才凑出来的。
“说呀！”克莱尔说。
“这怎么说，达德利？”韦瑟比夫人说。
“我可得说一句，皮克林，”韦瑟比勋爵说，“那畜生我再不喜欢，这也未免太过了！”
皮克林先生在那一片控诉的目光前连连后退。

===Original===
“Good heavens! Do you think I did it?”
In the midst of his anguish there flashed across his mind the recollection of having seen just this sort of situation in a moving picture and of having thought it far-fetched.
Lady Wetherby’s good-tempered mouth, far from good-tempered now, curled in a devastating sneer. She was looking at him as Claire, in the old days when they had toured England together in road companies, had sometimes seen her look at recalcitrant landladies. The landladies, without exception, had wilted beneath that gaze, and Mr. Pickering wilted now.
“But﻿—but﻿—but﻿—” was all he could contrive to say.
“Why should we think you did it?” said Lady Wetherby bitingly. “You had a grudge against the poor brute for biting you. We find you hiding here with a pistol and a story about burglars which an infant couldn’t swallow. I suppose you thought that, if you planted the poor creature’s body here, it would be up to Algie to get rid of it, and that if he were found with it I should think that it was he who had killed the animal.”
The look of horror which Lord Wetherby had managed to assume became genuine at these words. The gratitude which he had been feeling toward Mr. Pickering for having removed one of the chief trials of his existence vanished.

===Chinese===
“老天爷！你们当是我干的？”
痛苦之中，他脑际蓦地闪过一个记忆：曾在一部电影里见过恰恰眼前这个局面，当时还嫌它编得离谱。
韦瑟比夫人那张素来好脾气的嘴——此刻离好脾气远得很——撇出一副摧枯拉朽的冷笑。她望着他的那副眼神，从前克莱尔是见惯的：当年她们随巡回剧团跑遍英格兰，她有时便这样望那些不驯顺的房东太太。那些房东太太无一例外，在那目光下统统蔫了；皮克林先生如今也蔫了。
“可是——可是——可是——”他所能挤出来的，尽于此矣。
“我们凭什么当你干的？”韦瑟比夫人字字带刺。“它咬过你，你记恨这可怜的畜生。我们撞见你揣着枪躲在这儿，还编了个连吃奶娃娃都咽不下去的贼故事。我想你打的算盘是：把这可怜东西的尸首栽在这儿，打扫就成了阿尔吉的差事；他一旦同它一起被人发现，我自然当他就是杀死这畜生的凶手。”
韦瑟比勋爵方才好不容易装扮起来的那副惊骇之色，一听这话便成了真情实感。皮克林先生替他拔除了他此生大患之一，他本存着几分感激；这感激霎时化为乌有。

===Original===
“Great Scott!” he cried. “So that was the game, was it!”
Mr. Pickering struggled for speech. This was a nightmare.
“But I didn’t! I didn’t! I didn’t! I tell you I hadn’t the remotest notion the creature was there.”
“Oh, come, Pickering!” said Lord Wetherby. “Come, come, come!”
Mr. Pickering found that his accusers were ebbing away. Lady Wetherby had gone. Claire had gone. Only Lord Wetherby remained, looking at him like a pained groom. He dashed from the place and followed his hostess, speaking incoherently of burglars, outhouses and misunderstandings. He even mentioned Chingachgook. But Lady Wetherby would not listen. Nobody would listen.

===Chinese===
“我的老天！”他嚷道，“原来耍的是这个把戏，是不是！”
皮克林先生挣扎着要说话。这简直是一场噩梦。
“可我没干！没干！没干！我告诉你，那畜生在那儿，我连一丝一毫都不知道。”
“哦，得了，皮克林！”韦瑟比勋爵说。“得了，得了，得了！”
皮克林先生发觉，控诉他的人马正纷纷退去。韦瑟比夫人走了。克莱尔走了。只剩下韦瑟比勋爵，像一名伤透了心的马夫那样望着他。他冲出那地方，追着女主人，语无伦次地说什么窃贼、棚屋和误会。他甚至提起了金加古。可韦瑟比夫人听不进去。谁也不听。

===Original===
He found Lord Wetherby at his side, evidently prepared to go deeper into the subject. Lord Wetherby was looking now like a groom whose favorite horse has kicked him in the stomach.
“Wouldn’t have thought it of you, Pickering,” said Lord Wetherby. Mr. Pickering found no words. “Wouldn’t, honestly. Low trick!”
“But I tell you﻿—”
“Devilish low trick!” repeated Lord Wetherby, with a shake of the head. “Laws of hospitality﻿—eaten our bread and salt, what!﻿—all that sort of thing﻿—kill valuable monkey﻿—not done, you know﻿—low, very low!”
And he followed his wife, now in full retreat, with scorn and repulsion written in her very walk.
“Mr. Pickering!”

===Chinese===
他发觉韦瑟比勋爵已到了他的身侧，显然打算就本题再深谈一层。韦瑟比勋爵这时的神气，像一名心爱的马朝他肚子上踢了一蹄子的马夫。
“真没想到你会来这一手，皮克林，”韦瑟比勋爵说。皮克林先生找不出话来。“实在没想到。下流行径！”
“可我告诉你——”
“万恶的下流行径！”韦瑟比勋爵摇着头重复道。“待客的大道理——吃了我们的面包和盐，是不是！——这类的规矩——害死名贵的猴子——不像话，你知道的——下流，太下流！”
说罢他也追他妻子去了——她此刻正全线撤退，连步态本身都写满了轻蔑与嫌恶。
“皮克林先生！”

===Original===
It was Claire. She stood there, holding something toward him, something that glittered in the moonlight. Her voice was hard and the expression on her face suggested that in her estimation he was a particularly low-grade worm, one of the submerged tenth of the worm world.
“Eh?” said Mr. Pickering dazedly.
He looked at what she had in her hand, but it conveyed nothing to his overwrought mind.
“Take it!”

===Chinese===
是克莱尔。她站在那里，朝他递着一样东西，一样在月光下闪闪发亮的东西。她声音生硬，她脸上的神情则表明：在她眼中，他是一条品级尤其低下的虫子，虫界沉沦的十分之一里的一分子。
“啊？”皮克林先生昏头昏脑地问。
他望着她手里的东西，可他那段绷过头的神经对它毫无领会。
“拿去！”

===Original===
“Eh?”
Claire stamped.
“Very well,” she said.
She flung something on the ground before him, a small, sparkling object. Then she swept away, his eyes following her, and was lost in the darkness of the trees. Mechanically Mr. Pickering stooped to pick up what she had let fall. He recognized it now. It was her engagement ring.

===Chinese===
“啊？”
克莱尔一跺脚。
“那好。”她说。
她把一样东西摔在他面前的地上，一件小小的、晶光闪闪的物件。随后她拂袖而去，他的目光一路追着她，直到她没入树木的黑影。皮克林先生机械地俯下身去，拾起她掷下的东西。他这才认出来。是她的订婚戒指。

## XX / 第二十章

===Original===

In the smoking-room of Lady Wetherby’s house, chewing the dead stump of a once imposing cigar, Dudley Pickering sat alone with his thoughts. He had been alone for half an hour now. Once Lord Wetherby had looked in, to withdraw at once coldly, with the expression of a groom who has found loathsome things in the harness room. Roscoe Sherriff, good, easy man, who could never dislike people no matter what they had done, had come for a while to bear him company; but Mr. Pickering’s society was not for the time being entertaining. He had answered with grunts the press agent’s kindly attempts at conversation, and the latter had withdrawn to seek a more congenial audience. And now Mr. Pickering was alone, talking things over with his subconscious self.

A man’s subconscious self is not the ideal companion. It lurks for the greater part of his life in some dark den of its own, hidden away, and emerges only to taunt and deride and increase the misery of a miserable hour. Mr. Pickering’s rare interviews with his subconscious self had happened until now almost entirely in the small hours of the night, when it had popped out to remind him, as he lay sleepless, that all flesh was grass and that he was not getting any younger. Tonight, such had been the shock of the evening’s events, it came to him at a time which was usually his happiest, the time that lay between dinner and bed. Mr. Pickering at that point of the day was generally feeling his best. But tonight was different from the other nights of his life.

One may picture Subconscious Self as a withered, cynical, malicious person standing before Mr. Pickering and regarding him with an evil smile. There has been a pause, and now Subconscious Self speaks again.

===Chinese===

在韦瑟比夫人宅邸的吸烟室里，达德利·皮克林嚼着一截早已咽气的残蒂——那雪茄当年何等威风——独自坐着，同自己的心事打交道。他已独自待了半个钟头。韦瑟比勋爵来探头望过一回，随即冷冷地退了出去，一脸马夫在挽具房里撞见腌臜物事的表情。罗斯科·谢里夫是个厚道好说话的人，无论人家干过什么，他对谁都讨厌不起来，也来陪了他一阵；只是皮克林先生此刻做伴实在乏味。宣传经纪人好意搭话，他一概以哼哼作答，谢里夫便抽身去寻更有情趣的听众了。如今皮克林先生孤身一人，正同自己的潜意识合计事情。

人的潜意识算不得理想的侣伴。它一辈子大半光阴潜伏在自己那处黑暗巢穴里，藏而不露；非到凄凉的时辰绝不出头，出了头便只有讥诮、嘲弄，给凄凉的时辰再添一层凄凉。皮克林先生同潜意识难得会晤，迄今几乎全在夜深的小时里：那时它冷不丁蹿出来，提醒辗转难眠的他，凡有血气的尽都如草，而他也不比从前年轻了。今晚，晚间的事变故得太大，它竟在他素来最快活的时辰来了——晚饭后、就寝前的那段光景。一天到这个时分，皮克林先生照例浑身最是惬意。可今晚不同，同他此生的任何一夜都不同。

不妨把潜意识想象成一个干瘪、乖张、心怀歹意的人物，正立在皮克林先生面前，挂着一脸奸笑打量他。冷场片刻，如今潜意识又开口了。

===Original===

“You’ll have to leave tomorrow. Couldn’t possibly stop on after what’s happened. Now you see what comes of behaving like a boy.”

Mr. Pickering writhed.

“Made a pretty considerable fool of yourself, didn’t you, with your revolvers and your hidings and your trailings? Too old for that sort of thing, you know. You’re getting on. Probably have a touch of lumbago tomorrow. You must remember you aren’t a youngster. Got to take care of yourself. Next time you feel an impulse to hide in shrubberies and take moonlight walks through damp woods, perhaps you will listen to me.”

Mr. Pickering relit the stump of his cigar defiantly and smoked in long gulps for a while. He was trying to persuade himself that all this was untrue, but it was not easy. The cigar became uncomfortably hot, and he threw it away. He fumbled in his waistcoat pocket and produced a diamond ring, at which he looked pensively.

“A pretty thing, is it not?” said Subconscious Self, like a disembodied Eddie Foy.

===Chinese===

“你明天就得动身。出了这样的事，断没有再待下去的道理。这下你总瞧见了，孩子脾气是什么下场。”

皮克林先生扭怩不安。

“你出了好大一个洋相，不是么？挎着左轮手枪，又是藏匿，又是尾随？你也知道，这个岁数干不来这个了。你是往老里去了。明儿八成要闹一点腰痛。你须记住自己不是小伙子了。得保重自己。下回你再觉得心头一痒，想往灌木丛里躲、踏着月光穿潮乎乎的树林子散步，也许就肯听我的话了。”

皮克林先生不服气地把残蒂重新点上，长一口大一口地吞咽了好一阵。他竭力想说服自己这一切并非实情，可谈何容易。雪茄热得烫手，他一扬手丢了。他往马甲口袋里摸索，掏出一枚钻石戒指，对着它出起神来。

“好一件俏玩意儿，是不是？”潜意识说，口吻活脱是一个脱了躯壳的埃迪·福伊（Eddie Foy）。

===Original===

Mr. Pickering sighed. That moment when Claire had thrown the ring at his feet and swept out of his life like an offended queen had been the culminating blow of a night of blows, the knockout following on a series of minor punches. Subconscious Self seized the opportunity to become offensive again.

“You’ve lost her, all through your own silly fault,” it said, discarding Eddie Foy and adopting in preference the conversational methods of the late Bildad the Shuhite. “How on earth you can have been such a perfect fool beats me. Running round with a gun like a boy of fourteen! Well, it’s done now and it can’t be mended. Countermand the order for cake, send a wire putting off the minister, dismiss the bridesmaids, tell the organist he can stop practicing ‘The Voice That Breathed O’er Eden’﻿—no wedding bells for you. For Dudley Damfool Pickering, Esquire, the lonely hearth forevermore. Little feet pattering about the house? Not on your life! Childish voices sticking up the old man for half a dollar to buy candy? No, sir! Not for D. Bonehead Pickering, the amateur trailing arbutus!”

Subconscious Self may have had an undesirable way of expressing itself, but there was no denying the truth of what it said. Its words carried conviction. Mr. Pickering replaced the ring in his pocket and, burying his head in his hands, groaned in bitterness of spirit.

===Chinese===

皮克林先生长叹一声。克莱尔把戒指摔在他脚边，如一位受了冒犯的王后拂袖而去，从此绝迹于他的生命——那一刻，是这一夜连番打击里登峰造极的一记，是紧随一串轻拳之后的终局击倒。潜意识抓住机会，又放肆起来。

“她叫你给丢了，全怪你自己蠢得没边，”它说。它撇下了埃迪·福伊，改而拾起已故的书亚人比勒达（Bildad the Shuhite）的谈话路数。“你怎么能蠢到这般炉火纯青的地步，我实在想不通。挎着一杆枪四处乱窜，活像十四岁的娃娃！罢了，事已至此，无可挽回。把订好的喜糕退掉，拍电报辞了牧师，遣散伴娘，告诉风琴师《伊甸园上飘过的声音》不必再练了——你的婚礼钟声，吹了。献给‘糊涂蛋’达德利·皮克林先生：冷灶孤火，直到永远。小脚丫在屋里啪嗒啪嗒地跑？想都别想！娃娃嗓音缠着老头子敲五毛钱买糖吃？没门儿，先生！这种福气轮不到‘榆木脑袋’D·皮克林——咱们这位业余跟踪郎、‘匍匐熊果’（trailing arbutus 本是一种匍匐爬地的野花，trailing 兼指‘尾行跟踪’）！”

潜意识的谈吐或许要不得，可它说的道理却无从否认。字字入理，句句服人。皮克林先生把戒指收回口袋，双手抱头，辛酸地呻吟起来。

===Original===

He had lost her. He must face the fact. She had thrown him over. Never now would she sit at his table, the brightest jewel of Detroit’s glittering social life. She would have made a stir in Detroit. Now that city would never know her. Not that he was worrying much about Detroit. He was worrying about himself. How could he ever live without her?

This mood of black depression endured for a while, and then Mr. Pickering suddenly became aware that Subconscious Self was sneering at him.

“You’re a wonder!” said Subconscious Self.

“What do you mean?”

“Why, trying to make yourself think that at the bottom of your heart you aren’t tickled to death that this has happened. You know perfectly well that you’re tremendously relieved that you haven’t got to marry the girl, after all. You can fool everybody else, but you can’t fool me. You’re delighted, man, delighted!”

===Chinese===

他失去了她。他必须直面这个事实。她把他甩了。往后她再不会坐上他的餐桌，做底特律煊赫社交界顶明艳的一颗珠宝了。她本可以在底特律闹出个名堂。如今那座城永远无缘识得她。倒不是他有多替底特律惋惜。他惋惜的是自己。没了她，往后的日子可怎么过？

这阵乌云也似的深重抑郁盘桓了片刻，随后皮克林先生蓦地觉察，潜意识在朝他冷笑。

“你可真是个宝贝！”潜意识说。

“你什么意思？”

“嗐，你在拼命哄自己，说打心眼里你并不为这事乐得要死。你心里雪亮：到底不必娶这姑娘了，你如释重负，松快极了。旁人你骗得过，我可骗不了。你高兴着呢，老兄，高兴着呢！”

===Original===

The mere suggestion revolted Mr. Pickering. He was on the point of indignant denial, when quite abruptly there came home to him the suspicion that the statement was not so preposterous after all. It seemed incredible and indecent that such a thing should be, but he could not deny, now that it was put to him point-blank in this way, that a certain sense of relief was beginning to mingle itself with his gloom. It was shocking to realize, but﻿—yes, he actually was feeling as if he had escaped from something which he had dreaded. Half an hour ago there had been no suspicion of such an emotion among the many which had occupied his attention, but now he perceived it clearly. Half an hour ago he had felt like Lucifer hurled from heaven. Now, though how that train of thought had started he could not have said, he was distinctly conscious of the silver lining. Subconscious Self began to drive the thing home.

“Be honest with yourself,” it said. “You aren’t often. No man is. Look at the matter absolutely fairly. You know perfectly well that the mere idea of marriage has always scared you. You hate making yourself conspicuous in public. Think what it would be like, standing up there in front of all the world and getting married. And then﻿—afterward! Why on earth do you think that you would have been happy with this girl? What do you know about her except that she is a beauty? I grant you she’s that, but are you aware of the infinitesimal part looks play in married life? My dear chap, better is it for a man that he marry a sympathetic gargoyle than a Venus with a streak of hardness in her. You know﻿—and you would admit it if you were honest with yourself﻿—that this girl is hard. She’s got a chilled-steel soul.

“If you wanted to marry someone﻿—and there’s no earthly reason why you should, for your life’s perfectly full and happy with your work﻿—this is the last girl you ought to marry. You’re a middle-aged man. You’re set. You like life to jog along at a peaceful walk. This girl wants it to be a foxtrot. You’ve got habits which you have had for a dozen years. I ask you, is she the sort of girl to be content to be a stepmother to a middle-aged man’s habits? Of course if you were really in love with her, if she were your mate, and all that sort of thing, you would take a pleasure in making yourself over to suit her requirements. But you aren’t in love with her. You are simply caught by her looks. I tell you, you ought to look on that moment when she gave you back your ring as the luckiest moment of your life. You ought to make a sort of anniversary of it. You ought to endow a hospital or something out of pure gratitude. I don’t know how long you’re going to live﻿—if you act like a grown-up man instead of a boy and keep out of woods and shrubberies at night, you may live forever﻿—but you will never have a greater bit of luck than the one that happened to you tonight.”

===Chinese===

单是这一提法就叫皮克林先生起反感。他正要愤然驳斥，却蓦地心头一动，疑心这话究竟也未必尽属荒唐。世事竟至如此，叫人难以置信，也叫人于心不安；可话既这样劈面点破，他便无从否认：一缕如释重负之感，正开始渗进他的愁云。意识到这一点实在骇人，可是——不错，他的确觉得自己仿佛逃开了一向惧怕的什么东西。半个钟头之前，盘踞他心头的诸般情绪里，丝毫不见这一味的踪影；如今他却看得清清楚楚。半个钟头之前，他还自比堕入深渊的路西法（Lucifer）；此刻——这条思绪从哪条岔道拐过来的，他自己也说不清——他分明望见了乌云镶的银边。潜意识开始穷追不舍，把话往实里敲。

“同你自己说句老实话吧，”它说，“你难得这样。男人都难得。把这桩事放上天平，公公正正掂一掂。你心里雪亮，‘结婚’这两个字，念头一起，向来就够吓住你的。你厌恶在大庭广众之间惹眼。想一想吧，当着普天之下站在那儿行婚礼，是什么光景。还有往后——婚后的日子！你凭什么认定，同这姑娘过你就能幸福？撇开她是个美人这一点，你对她又知道些什么？美人我认账，可你明白容貌在婚姻生活里占的分量有多微末么？我的好人哪，男人娶一尊善体人意的石像鬼（gargoyle），也强似娶一位心肠硬上半分的维纳斯。你知道——你若肯同自己说句老实话，也自会承认——这姑娘心肠是硬的。她的灵魂是淬过火的钢。”

“就算你想娶个什么人——其实天底下没有半点理由非娶不可，你的日子有工作撑着，充实圆满，其乐也无穷——这姑娘也是你顶顶娶不得的一个。你是个中年人了。性子已定。你喜欢日子迈着四平八稳的步子遛达。这姑娘却要它跳狐步舞（foxtrot）。你有一套积了十几年的老习惯。我问你，她可是那种甘心给一个中年人的习惯当继母的姑娘？自然，你若真心爱她，她若真是你的天造地设，诸如此类，那么照她的要求把自己回炉改造一番，你也会乐在其中。可你并不爱她。你不过是叫她的姿色拿住了。听我说，她把戒指还你那一刻，你该当作一生顶走运的时刻。你该把它立成个周年纪念日。你该出于纯粹的感恩，捐上一所医院什么的。我不知道你还能活多久——你若肯拿出大人的样子，别再孩子气，夜里不往树林子和灌木丛里钻，你兴许能长生不老——可今晚落到你头上的这注好运，是你此生再不会有的了。”

===Original===

Mr. Pickering was convinced. His spirits soared. Marriage! What was marriage? Slavery, not to be endured by your man of spirit. Look at all the unhappy marriages you saw everywhere. Besides, you had only to recall some of the novels and plays of recent years to get the right angle on marriage. According to the novelists and playwrights, shrewd fellows who knew what was what, if you talked to your wife about your business she said you had no soul; if you didn’t she said you didn’t think enough of her to let her share your life. If you gave her expensive presents and an unlimited credit account she complained that you looked on her as a mere doll; and if you didn’t she called you a tightwad. What was marriage? If it didn’t get you with the left jab it landed on you with the right uppercut. None of that sort of thing for Dudley Pickering.

“You’re absolutely right,” he said enthusiastically. “Funny I never looked at it that way before.”

Somebody was turning the door handle. He hoped it was Roscoe Sherriff. He had been rather dull the last time Sherriff had looked in. He would be quite different now. He would be gay and sparkling. He remembered two good Ford stories he would like to tell Sherriff.

The door opened and Claire came in. There was a silence. She stood looking at him in a way that puzzled Mr. Pickering. If it had not been for her attitude at their last meeting and the manner in which she had broken that last meeting up, he would have said that her look seemed somehow to strike a note of appeal. There was something soft and repentant about her. She suggested, it seemed to Mr. Pickering, the prodigal daughter revisiting the old homestead.

===Chinese===

皮克林先生信服了。他心气陡然高扬。婚姻！婚姻算个什么？是奴役，但凡有血性的人都消受不起。睁开眼看看四下里，不幸的婚姻还少么。再说，只消把近几年的几部小说和戏剧回想一回，婚姻的正经角度便到手了。照小说家和剧作家——那班精明老到、深谙世情底细的家伙——的说法：你同妻子谈自己的买卖，她说你没有灵魂；你不谈，她又说你不把她放在眼里，不肯让她分担你的生活。你送她贵重礼物、给她开一个不限额的赊账户头，她抱怨你只把她当个洋娃娃；你不送，她又骂你是铁公鸡。婚姻是什么？左刺拳搆不着你，右上勾拳也准要落在你身上。这一路玩意儿，达德利·皮克林一概敬谢不敏。

“你说得千真万确，”他热切地说，“怪就怪在，从前我怎么看也不往这一层想。”

有人在转动门把。他巴不得来的是罗斯科·谢里夫。上一回谢里夫来看他，他沉闷得很。这一回可就大不相同了。他要谈笑风生，妙语连珠。他记起两段顶好的福特（Ford）笑话，正想讲给谢里夫听。

门开了，克莱尔走了进来。一阵静默。她立在那里望着他，那神情叫皮克林先生捉摸不透。若不是上一回见面时她的做派、连同她把那场见面揽散的方式历历在目，他简直要说，她的目光里透着一缕近乎哀恳的调子。她身上有种柔软的、悔艾的意味。在皮克林先生看来，她像极了浪子回头、重返旧宅的故事——只是这一回，回头的换成了女儿家。

===Original===

“Dudley!”

She smiled a faint smile, a wistful, deprecating smile. She was looking lovelier than ever. Her face glowed with a wonderful color and her eyes were very bright. Mr. Pickering met her gaze, and strange things began to happen to his mind, that mind which a moment before had thought so clearly and established so definite a point of view.

What a gelatin-backboned thing is man, who prides himself on his clear reason and becomes as wet blotting-paper at one glance from bright eyes! A moment before, Mr. Pickering had thought out the whole subject of woman and marriage in a few bold flashes of his capable brain, and thanked Providence that he was not as those men who take unto themselves wives to their undoing. Now in an instant he had lost that iron outlook. Reason was temporarily out of business. He was slipping.

“Dudley!”

===Chinese===

“达德利！”

她浅浅一笑，那笑里含着眷念，又带着歉怯。她比以往哪一回都更好看。满脸焕发着异样的光彩，一双眼睛亮极了。皮克林先生迎着她的目光，怪事开始在他脑子里发生了——就在片刻之前，这副脑子还想得那样清明，立下过那样斩钉截铁的见解。

人这东西，脊梁骨原是明胶做的：方才还自诩理性清明，叫明亮的眼睛一瞥，登时成了一张湿透的吸墨纸！片刻之前，皮克林先生那颗干练的脑子几道闪电，把女人与婚姻这整个题目想了个通透，还感谢上苍，自己不同那班娶妻娶来祸殃的男人为伍。转眼之间，那副铁打的定见丢了个干净。理性临时歇了业。他正在往下溜。

“达德利！”

===Original===

For a space Subconscious Self thrust itself forward.

“Look out! Be careful!” it warned.

Mr. Pickering ignored it. He was watching, fascinated, the glow on Claire’s face, her shining eyes.

“Dudley, I want to speak to you.”

“Tell her you can only be seen by appointment! Escape! Bolt!”

Mr. Pickering did not bolt. Claire came toward him, still smiling that pathetic smile. A thrill permeated Mr. Pickering’s entire one hundred and ninety-seven pounds, trickling down his spine like hot water and coming out at the soles of his feet. He had forgotten now that he had ever sneered at marriage. It seemed to him now that there was nothing in life to be compared with that beatific state, and that bachelors were mere wild asses of the desert.

===Chinese===

有一阵子，潜意识抢到了前头。

“当心！留神！”它警告道。

皮克林先生没有理会。他望着克莱尔脸上的红晕、那双亮闪闪的眼睛，望得出了神。

“达德利，我有话要同你说。”

“告诉她，你只按预约接见！快逃！撒腿跑！”

皮克林先生没有撒腿。克莱尔朝他走来，依然挂着那副楚楚可怜的笑。一阵战栗浸透了皮克林先生全身上下一百九十七磅的家当，像热水顺着脊梁骨往下淌，从脚底板淌了出去。此刻他早忘了自己讥诮过婚姻这回事。如今在他看来，人世间没有一样东西比得上那至福的境界，而单身汉们不过是旷野里的野驴罢了。

===Original===

Claire came and sat down on the arm of his chair. He moved convulsively, but he stayed where he was.

“Fool!” said Subconscious Self.

Claire took hold of his hand and patted it. He quivered, but remained.

“Ass!” hissed Subconscious Self.

Claire stopped patting his hand and began to stroke it. Mr. Pickering breathed heavily.

“Dudley, dear,” said Claire softly, “I’ve been an awful fool, and I’m dreadfully, dreadfully sorry, and you’re going to be the nicest, kindest, sweetest man on earth and tell me you’ve forgiven me. Aren’t you?”

===Chinese===

克莱尔走过来，在他椅子的扶手上坐下了。他痉挛似的一动，人却没有挪窝。

“蠢货！”潜意识说。

克莱尔握住他的手，轻轻拍着。他哆嗦了一下，人还留在原地。

“笨驴！”潜意识嘶声道。

克莱尔不拍了，改而摩挲起他的手来。皮克林先生呼吸粗重。

“达德利，亲爱的，”克莱尔柔声说，“我从前是个天大的傻瓜，我难受极了，难受极了，你呢，就要做天底下顶好、顶厚道、顶贴心的人，告诉我你原谅我了。是不是？”

===Original===

Mr. Pickering’s lips moved silently. Claire kissed the thinning summit of his head. There was a pause.

“Where is it?” she asked.

Mr. Pickering started.

“Eh?”

“Where is it? Where did you put it? The ring, silly!”

Mr. Pickering became aware that Subconscious Self was addressing him. The occasion was tense and Subconscious Self did not mince its words.

===Chinese===

皮克林先生的嘴唇无声地翕动。克莱尔吻了吻他头顶那日渐稀疏的峰巅。一阵停顿。

“东西呢？”她问。

皮克林先生吓了一跳。

“嗯？”

“东西呢？你搁哪儿了？戒指呀，傻瓜！”

皮克林先生这才觉察，潜意识是在同他讲话。此刻情势紧张，潜意识说话也就毫不含糊。

===Original===

“You poor, maudlin, sentimental, doddering chunk of imbecility,” it said; “are there no limits to your insanity? After all I said to you just now, are you deliberately going to start the old idiocy all over again?”

“She’s so beautiful,” pleaded Mr. Pickering. “Look at her eyes!”

“Ass! Don’t you remember what I said about beauty?”

“Yes, I know, but﻿—”

“She’s as hard as nails.”

===Chinese===

“你这坨可怜见的、哭哭啼啼、多愁善感、老迈昏聩的大蠢肉，”它说，“你的疯病就没有个边么？我方才同你说的那一大篇，你存心要把那套老白痴戏码从头再来一遍？”

“她那么美，”皮克林先生哀求道，“瞧瞧她的眼睛！”

“笨驴！我说过容貌怎么着，你不记得了？”

“记得，我知道，可是——”

“她心肠硬得跟钉子一个样。”

===Original===

“I’m sure you’re wrong.”

“I’m not wrong.”

“But she loves me.”

“Forget it!”

Claire jogged his shoulders.

“Dudley, dear, what are you sitting there dreaming for? Where did you put the ring?”

===Chinese===

“我敢保你错了。”

“我没有错。”

“可她爱我。”

“拉倒吧！”

克莱尔推了推他的肩膀。

“达德利，亲爱的，你坐在那儿出什么神呢？戒指搁哪儿了？”

===Original===

Mr. Pickering fumbled for it, located it, produced it. Claire examined it fondly.

“Did she throw it at him and nearly break his heart!” she said.

“Bolt!” urged Subconscious Self. “Fly! Go to Japan!”

Mr. Pickering did not go to Japan. He was staring worshipingly at Claire. With rapturous gaze he noted the gray glory of her eyes, the delicate curve of her cheek, the grace of her neck. He had no time to listen to pessimistic warnings from any Gloomy Gus of a Subconscious Self. He was ashamed that he had ever even for a moment allowed himself to be persuaded that Claire was not all that was perfect. No more doubts and hesitations for Dudley Pickering. He was under the influence.

“There!” said Claire, and slipped the ring on her finger.

She kissed the top of his head once more.

===Chinese===

皮克林先生摸索了一阵，摸着了方位，掏了出来。克莱尔一往情深地端详着。

“瞧她那会儿把它往人家身上一摔，差点儿没把人家的心摔碎！”她说。

“快逃！”潜意识敦促道，“飞啊！逃去日本！”

皮克林先生没有去日本。他一脸虔敬，凝望着克莱尔。狂喜的目光里，他描摹着她灰眼睛里那片荣光、她面颊柔婉的曲线、她颈项的雅致。潜意识里随便哪个丧气鬼（Gloomy Gus）发出来的悲观警告，他也没工夫听了。他满心羞惭：自己居然一度、哪怕只是一时，信了克莱尔并非十全十美。怀疑与踌躇，从此同达德利·皮克林无缘。他着了道儿了。

“好了！”克莱尔说着，把戒指套上了自己的手指。

她又吻了吻他的头顶。

===Original===

“So there we are!” she said.

“There we are!” gurgled the infatuated Dudley.

“Happy now?”

“Ur-r!”

“Then kiss me.”

===Chinese===

“这下可成了！”她说。

“成了！成了！”神魂颠倒的达德利咕哝道。

“这会儿幸福了吧？”

“呃——嗯！”

“那就亲亲我。”

===Original===

Mr. Pickering kissed her.

“Dudley, darling,” said Claire, “we’re going to be awfully, awfully happy, aren’t we?”

“You bet we are!” said Mr. Pickering.

Subconscious Self said nothing, being beyond speech.

===Chinese===

皮克林先生吻了她。

“达德利，亲爱的，”克莱尔说，“我们会幸福极了，幸福极了，是不是？”

“那还用说！”皮克林先生说。

潜意识一言不发——它已经气得说不出话来了。

## XXI / 第二十一章

===Original===
For some minutes after Claire had left him Bill remained where he was, motionless. He felt physically incapable of moving. All the strength that was in him he was using to throw off the insidious poison of her parting speech, and it became plainer to him with each succeeding moment that he would have need of strength.

It is part of the general irony of things that in life’s crises a man’s good qualities are often the ones that help him least, if indeed they do not actually turn treacherously and fight against him. It was so with Bill. Modesty, if one may trust to the verdict of the mass of mankind, is a good quality. It sweetens the soul and makes for a kindly understanding of one’s fellows. But arrogance would have served Bill better now. It was his fatal habit of self-depreciation that was making Claire’s words so specious, as he stood there trying to cast them from his mind. Who was he, after all, that he should imagine that he had won on his personal merits a girl like Elizabeth Boyd?

He had never been able to look on himself, after the manner of many of the men he knew, as a tremendous fellow, the center of a wondering world, Nature’s supreme handiwork. He was conscious﻿—too conscious﻿—of his shortcomings. From boyhood the sort of man he had admired was the capable, dashing, quick-witted, debonair fellow, the man you could never take without his answer, whether you attacked him with a sword or with an epigram, and the realization of the gulf that separated himself from this ideal male had engendered humility, increasing with the years. He had the not very common type of mind that perceives the merit in others more readily than their faults and in himself the faults more readily than the merit. Time and the society of a great number of men of different ranks and natures had rid him of the outer symbol of this type of mind, which is shyness, but it had left him still unconvinced that he amounted to anything very much as an individual.

===Chinese===
克莱尔（Claire）离去之后，比尔（Bill）在原地待了好几分钟，一动不动。他只觉得浑身瘫软，动弹不得。他拿出了周身的气力，去驱除她临别那番话里暗中蚀人的毒；而一刻一刻过去，他益发分明地感到，这份气力往后还少不得要用。

世事通盘的嘲弄之中有一桩是：人生危急关头，一个人的种种美德往往最帮不上他的忙，有时甚至当真倒戈相向，反过来同他作对。比尔正是如此。谦逊，倘若可以信从世人的公断，是一种美德。它润泽灵魂，使人对自己的同类怀一份厚道的体谅。可此刻，狂妄自大反倒更能给比尔帮上忙。他站在那里，竭力想把那些话从心里撵出去，而叫克莱尔那些话显得如此似是而非的，正是他那要命的自贬习气。说到底，他是何等样人，竟敢妄想凭自身的长处赢得伊丽莎白·博伊德（Elizabeth Boyd）这样的姑娘？

他从来没能像他认识的许多男人那样，把自己看作一位了不得的人物——万众惊叹的世界以他为中心，他是大自然至高无上的手笔。他对自己的短处心知肚明——太心知肚明了。打从男童时代起，他所敬慕的便是那一路精明干练、骁捷争先、机敏过人、风流倜傥的角色——不论你用剑向他进击，还是用隽语向他讥刺，都休想制住他而听不到他还口；而一旦悟到自己与这位理想男子之间隔着一道鸿沟，谦卑便在他心里生了根，并且逐年滋长。他那种头脑属于不太多见的一型：看别人的长处比看别人的短处来得容易，看自己的短处又比看自己的长处来得容易。岁月，加上同许许多多出身不同、性情各异的男子交游往还，早已使他褪尽了这类头脑的外在表征——羞怯；却仍没能叫他相信，自己作为一个体，究竟算得上多重要的人物。

===Original===
This was the thought that met him every time he tried to persuade himself that what Claire had said was ridiculous, the mere parting shaft of an angry woman. With this thought as an ally her words took on a plausibility hard to withstand. Plausible! That was the devil of it. By no effort could he blind himself to the fact that they were that. In the light of Claire’s insinuations what had seemed coincidences took on a more sinister character. It had seemed to him an odd and lucky chance that Nutty Boyd should have come to the rooms which he was occupying that night, seeking a companion. Had it been chance? Even at the time he had thought it strange that, on the strength of a single evening spent together, Nutty should have invited a total stranger to make an indefinite visit at his home. Had there been design behind the invitation?

Bill began to walk slowly to the house. He felt tired and unhappy. He meant to go to bed and try to sleep away these wretched doubts and questionings. Daylight would bring relief.

As he reached the open front door he caught the sound of voices, and paused for an instant, almost unconsciously, to place them. They came from one of the rooms upstairs. It was Nutty speaking now, and it was impossible for Bill not to hear what he said, for Nutty had abandoned his customary drawl in favor of a high, excited tone.

===Chinese===
每逢他竭力说服自己——克莱尔那番话荒唐无稽，不过是一个恼怒的女人临别掷来的暗箭——总有这个念头迎面拦住他。有了这念头充当盟友，她的话便显出一种难以抵挡的貌似有理。貌似有理！坏就坏在这里。任凭他怎样使劲，他都无法闭上眼睛不承认：那些话确乎如此。经克莱尔那番含沙射影一映照，原先似乎只是巧合的种种事情，便染上了更不祥的色彩。那一晚，纳蒂·博伊德（Nutty Boyd）会跑到他落脚的那几间屋里来找伴儿，他向来觉得是一桩古怪而走运的偶然。那当真是偶然吗？单凭同处一晚的交情，纳蒂便邀请一个素昧平生的人到他家里不限时日地住下去，当时他就已经觉得蹊跷。这份邀请的背后，莫非另有布置？

比尔开始缓步朝屋子走去。他觉得又疲倦又难受。他打算上床睡下，想法子把这些凄惶的疑虑和诘问都在睡乡里睡化了。天光一亮，自会松快。

走到敞开着的前门口，他听见屋里有说话的声音，便几乎不由自主地驻足片刻，想辨出那是谁的声音。声音从楼上一个房间里传来。此刻说话的是纳蒂，而比尔想不听见他的话也办不到，因为纳蒂已经抛下惯常的拖腔，换上了又高又激动的调门。

===Original===
“Of course you hate him and all that,” said Nutty, “but after all you will be getting five million dollars that ought to have come to﻿—”

That was all that Bill heard, for he had stumbled across the hall and was in his room, sitting on the bed and staring into the darkness with burning eyes. The door banged behind him.

So it was true!

===Chinese===
“当然，你恨他，这一层且不去说它，”纳蒂说，“可说到底，那五百万美元就要落到你手里了，而这笔钱本来是应该——”

比尔听到的就只有这些，因为他已经跌跌撞撞地穿过了门厅，进了自己的房间，坐在床上，一双火烧的眼睛直瞪着黑暗。房门在他身后砰地一声碰上了。

这么说，那是真的！

===Original===
There came a knock at the door. It was repeated. The handle turned.

“Is that you, Bill?”

It was Elizabeth’s voice. He could just see her, framed in the doorway.

“Bill!”

===Chinese===
有人敲门。又敲了一遍。门把手转动了。

“是你吗，比尔？”

是伊丽莎白的声音。他恰好望得见她，整个人像入了画框似的嵌在门口。

“比尔！”

===Original===
His throat was dry. Something seemed to be blocking it up. He swallowed, and found that he could speak.

“Yes?”

“Did you just come in?”

“Yes.”

===Chinese===
他喉咙发干，仿佛有什么东西堵在里头。他咽了一口，发觉自己能够说话了。

“嗯？”

“你是刚回来？”

“是的。”

===Original===
The door handle shook. Outside a whippoorwill had begun its monotonous cry. The sound seemed to beat on his brain like a hammer.

“Then﻿—you heard?”

“Yes.”

There was a long silence. Then the door closed gently and he heard her go upstairs.

===Chinese===
门把手在颤。屋外，一只三声夜鹰开始了一连串单调的啼鸣。那声音像一柄锤子，一下一下敲在他的脑际。

“那么——你听见了？”

“是的。”

好一阵沉默。随后，门轻轻地合上了，他听见她上楼去了。

## XXII / 第二十二章

===Original===
When Bill woke next morning it was ten o’clock; and his first emotion, on a day that was to be crowded with emotions of various kinds, was one of shame. The desire to do the fitting thing is innate in man, and it struck Bill, as he hurried through his toilet, that he must be a shallow, coarse-fibered sort of person, lacking in the finer feelings, not to have passed a sleepless night. There was something revolting in the thought that in circumstances which would have made sleep an impossibility for most men he had slept like a log. He did not do himself the justice to recollect that he had had a singularly strenuous day, and that it is Nature’s business, which she performs quietly and unromantically, to send sleep to tired men, regardless of their private feelings; and it was in a mood of dissatisfaction with the quality of his soul that he left his room. He had a general feeling that he was not much of a chap and that when he died﻿—which he trusted would be shortly﻿—the world would be well rid of him. He felt humble and depressed and hopeless.

Elizabeth met him in the passage. At the age of eleven or thereabouts women acquire a poise and an ability to handle difficult situations which a man, if he is lucky, manages to achieve somewhere in the later seventies.

Except for a pallor strange to her face, and a drawn look about the eyes, there was nothing to show that all was not for the best with Elizabeth in a best of all possible worlds. If she did not look jaunty she at least looked composed. She greeted Bill with a smile.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨比尔醒来时，已是十点钟；这一天注定要有形形色色的情绪轮番登场，而他头一样情绪，是羞愧。把事情办得得体，是人与生俱来的心愿；比尔匆匆梳洗的时候便暗想，自己昨夜竟没有辗转难眠，想必是个浅薄皮糙、缺少细腻感情的家伙。一想到换个人处在这种境地多半要彻夜无眠，自己却睡得像段木头，他就觉得可憎可厌。他倒没替自己说句公道话：他前一天实在辛苦非常，而大自然的天职便是把睡眠送给疲惫的人——这差事她办得安安静静、毫无诗意，从不体恤各人的私情；他正是怀着这股对自身灵魂品质的不满走出房间的。他模模糊糊地觉得自己不大会做人，等他死掉——但愿那一天早些到来——世人正好图个清净。他只觉得自卑、消沉、绝望。

伊丽莎白在走廊里迎面遇上他。女人到了十一岁上下，便养出一种从容气度、一套应付为难局面的本事；男人就算运气好，也要熬到七十七八岁才勉强修得。

若不是她脸上带着一种少见的苍白，眼边一线困顿的倦色，任谁也看不出，在这个万事皆如人意的最好世界里，伊丽莎白竟有什么不顺遂。她算不上神色爽利，至少神色镇定。她微笑着同比尔打招呼。

===Original===
“I didn’t wake you. I thought I would let you sleep on.”

The words had the effect of lending an additional clarity and firmness of outline to the picture of himself which Bill had already drawn in his mind﻿—of a soulless creature sunk in hoggish slumber.

“We’ve had breakfast. Nutty has gone for a walk. Isn’t he wonderful nowadays! I’ve kept your breakfast warm for you.”

===Chinese===
“我没叫醒你。我想着让你睡下去。”

这话像是给比尔心里早已画好的那幅自画像又添了几分清晰与坚实的轮廓——一个没有灵魂的家伙，正沉在猪猡般的酣睡里。

“我们都吃过早饭了。纳蒂散步去了。他如今可真了不得！你的早饭我给你温着呢。”

===Original===
Bill protested. He might be capable of sleep, but he was not going to sink to food.

“Not for me, thanks,” he said hollowly.

“Come along.”

“Honestly﻿—”

“Come along.”

He followed her meekly. How grimly practical women were! They let nothing interfere with the essentials of life. It seemed all wrong. Nevertheless, he breakfasted well and gratefully, Elizabeth watching him in silence across the table.

===Chinese===
比尔连连推却。他或许堕落得能睡觉，可还不至于堕落到来吃饭。

“我不吃了，谢谢。”他声音发空地说。

“来吧。”

“我真的——”

“来吧。”

他乖乖地跟了去。女人是多么铁面实际啊！什么也休想妨碍生活里的正经大事。这总觉得有点不对。话虽如此，他还是美美吃了一顿早饭，心怀感激，伊丽莎白隔着桌子默默看着他。

===Original===
“Finished?”

“Yes, thanks.”

She hesitated for a moment.

“Well, Bill, I’ve slept on it. Things are in rather a muddle, aren’t they! I think I had better begin by explaining what led up to those words you heard Nutty say last night. Won’t you smoke?”

===Chinese===
“吃完了？”

“吃完了，谢谢。”

她迟疑了片刻。

“是这样，比尔，我睡了一夜，把事情想过了一遍。局面搅得相当乱，可不是么！我想最好先跟你讲一讲前因——你昨儿晚上听见纳蒂说的那番话，是怎么一步步来的。你不抽支烟么？”

===Original===
“No, thanks.”

“You’ll feel better if you do.”

“I couldn’t.”

===Chinese===
“不抽，谢谢。”

“抽了你会好受些。”

“我抽不下。”

===Original===
A bee had flown in through the open window. She followed it with her eye as it blundered about the room. It flew out again into the sunshine. She turned to Bill again.

“They were supposed to be words of consolation,” she said.

Bill said nothing.

“Nutty, you see, has his own peculiar way of looking at things, and it didn’t occur to him that I might have promised to marry you because I loved you. He took it for granted that I had done it to save the Boyd home. He has been very anxious from the first that I should marry you. I think that that must have been why he asked you down here. He found out in New York, you know, who you were. Someone you met at supper recognized you, and told Nutty. So, as far as that is concerned, the girl you were speaking to at the gate last night was right.”

===Chinese===
一只蜜蜂从敞开的窗户飞了进来。它满屋子乱撞，她的目光一路追着它。它又飞出去，回到阳光里。她重新转向比尔。

“那番话，原是安慰人来着。”她说。

比尔没有作声。

“你要知道，纳蒂看事情自有他独门的一套，他压根没想过，我答应嫁给你也许是因为我爱你。他认定我这么做是为了保住博伊德家的门户。打一开始，他就一心盼着我嫁给你。我猜他当初请你下到这儿来，多半就是这个缘故。他在纽约打听出了你的身份，你是知道的。夜宵席上有个人认出了你，告诉了纳蒂。所以单就这一层说，昨儿晚上在大门口同你说话的那个姑娘并没有说错。”

===Original===
He started.

“You heard her?”

“I couldn’t help it. She meant me to hear. She was raising her voice quite unnecessarily if she did not mean to include me in the conversation. I had gone in to find Nutty and he was out, and I was coming back to you. That’s how I was there. You didn’t see me because your back was turned. She saw me.”

===Chinese===
他一惊。

“你听见了？”

“我没法不听。她存心要叫我听见。她把嗓门抬得那么高，若不是存心把我算进这场谈话，未免高得没道理。我进屋去找纳蒂，他不在，我便回身来找你。我就是在那儿。你没瞧见我，因为你的背朝着那边。她瞧见我了。”

===Original===
Bill met her eyes.

“You don’t ask who she was?”

“It doesn’t matter who she was. It’s what she said that matters. She said that we knew you were Lord Dawlish.”

“Did you know?”

===Chinese===
比尔迎着她的目光。

“你不问问她是谁？”

“她是谁不打紧。要紧的是她说了什么。她说，我们早知道你是达利什勋爵。”

“你们知道么？”

===Original===
“Nutty told me two or three days ago.” Her voice shook and a flush came into her face. “You probably won’t believe it, but the news made absolutely no difference to me one way or the other. I had always imagined Lord Dawlish as a treacherous, adventurous sort of man, because I couldn’t see how a man who was not like that could have persuaded Uncle Ira to leave him his money. But after knowing you even for this short time, I knew you were quite the opposite of that, and I remembered that the first thing you had done on coming into the money had been to offer me half, so the information that you were the Lord Dawlish whom I had been hating did not affect me. And the fact that you were rich and I was poor did not affect me either. I loved you, and that was all I cared about. If all this had not happened everything would have been all right. But, you see, nine-tenths of what that girl said to you was so perfectly true that it is humanly impossible for you not to believe the other tenth, which wasn’t. And then, to clinch it, you hear Nutty consoling me. That brings me back to Nutty.”

===Chinese===
“纳蒂两三天前告诉我的。”她的声音发颤，脸上涌起一阵红晕。“你多半不会相信，可这个消息不论真假，对我没有半点影响。我从前一直把达利什勋爵想象成一种奸诈冒险之徒，因为我想不明白，一个人若不是那路人，怎么能让舅舅艾拉把遗产留给他。可是同你相识虽然才这么些日子，我已经知道你恰是那路人的反面，我还记得你一得到那笔钱，头一件事就是提出分我一半，所以，得知你就是我一直恨着的那个达利什勋爵，这消息并不动我心。至于你有钱我没钱，同样不在话下。我爱你，我所计较的只有这个。倘若这些事都没有出，一切本来好好的。可是你要明白，那姑娘对你说的十成里有九成千真万确，叫你没法不信剩下那一成不真的。跟着，为了钉牢这一注，你又听见纳蒂在安慰我。这就又绕回纳蒂身上了。”

===Original===
“I﻿—”

“Let me tell you about Nutty first. I said that he had always been anxious that I should marry you. Something happened last night to increase his anxiety. I have often wondered how he managed to get enough money to enable him to spend three days in New York, and last night he told me. He came in just after I had got back to the house after leaving you and that girl, and he was very scared. It seems that when the letter from the London lawyer came telling him that he had been left a hundred dollars, he got the idea of raising money on the strength of it. You know Nutty by this time, so you won’t be surprised at the way he went about it. He borrowed a hundred dollars from the man at the drugstore on the security of that letter, and then﻿—I suppose it seemed so easy that it struck him as a pity to let the opportunity slip﻿—he did the same thing with four other tradesmen. Nutty’s so odd that I don’t know even now whether it ever occurred to him that he was obtaining money under false pretenses; but the poor tradesmen hadn’t any doubt about it at all. They compared notes and found what had happened, and last night, while we were in the woods, one of them came here and called Nutty a good many names and threatened him with the penitentiary.

“You can imagine how delighted Nutty was when I came in and told him that I was engaged to you. In his curious way he took it for granted that I had heard about his financial operations, and was doing it entirely for his sake, to get him out of his fix. And while I was trying to put him right on that point he began to console me. You see, Nutty looks on you as the enemy of the family, and it didn’t strike him that it was possible that I didn’t look on you in that light too. So, after being delighted for a while, he very sweetly thought that he ought to cheer me up and point out some of the compensations of marriage with you. And﻿—well, that was what you heard. There you have the full explanation. You can’t possibly believe it.”

===Chinese===
“我——”

“让我先讲纳蒂的事。我说过，他打头起就一心盼着我嫁给你。昨儿夜里出了一件事，叫他这份心思愈发急了。他这三天的纽约是怎么凑出盘缠来的，我素来纳闷，昨儿夜里他招了。我离开你和那个姑娘回到屋里，他脚跟着就进来了，吓得不轻。原来伦敦律师的信寄到，说他得了一百美元的遗产，他就起了个念头，要凭这封信去筹钱。纳蒂的为人你到这会儿也摸熟了，他行事的路数再不会叫你吃惊。他拿那封信作保，从药房老板那里借了一百美元，跟着——我猜此事既然如此顺手，眼看机会溜走未免可惜——又照方抓药，向另外四家商铺如法炮制。纳蒂这个人古怪到这步田地：他当时究竟想到没想到自己这是在招摇撞骗，我如今也说不准；可怜那些做买卖的却是半点不疑心。他们彼此一对账，弄清了原委。昨儿夜里，正当我们在树林子里的时候，其中一个找上门来，把纳蒂好一顿臭骂，还扬言要送他蹲大牢。”

“你可以想见，我进屋告诉他我同你订了婚，纳蒂有多高兴。他以他那一套古怪逻辑想当然认定，我必定是听说了他那几桩财务把戏，此举全是为着他，要把他从困境里捞出来。正当我竭力纠正他这个念头，他却开口安慰起我来。你要明白，纳蒂把你当成全家的仇敌，他从没想到，我未必也拿你当仇敌看。于是他高兴了一阵之后，又十二分体贴地认为该给我打打气，指出几条嫁给你也不无补偿的地方。还有——呃，你听见的就是这些。前因后果全在这里。你决计没法相信的。”

===Original===
She broke off and began to drum her fingers on the table. And as she did so there came to Bill a sudden relief from all the doubts and black thoughts that had tortured him. Elizabeth was straight. Whatever appearances might seem to suggest, nothing could convince him that she was playing an underhand game. It was as if something evil had gone out of him. He felt lighter, cleaner. He could breathe.

“I do believe it,” he said. “I believe every word you say.”

She shook her head.

===Chinese===
她戛然收住，手指头在桌上敲起鼓来。就在这时，折磨了比尔许久的种种疑云与黑念头忽然一泄而空。伊丽莎白是清白的。无论表象摆出什么样子，任什么也不能叫他相信她在暗中使什么手段。仿佛有什么脏东西从他身上泄了出去。他觉得轻了，干净了。他能够喘气了。

“我信。”他说，“你的话我字字都信。”

她摇头。

===Original===
“You can’t, in the face of the evidence.”

“I believe it.”

“No. You may persuade yourself for the moment that you do, but after a while you will have to go by the evidence. You won’t be able to help yourself. You haven’t realized what a crushing thing evidence is. You have to go by it against your will. You see, evidence is the only guide. You don’t know that I am speaking the truth, you just feel it. You’re trusting your heart and not your head. The head must win in the end. You might go on believing for a time, but sooner or later you would be bound to begin to doubt and worry and torment yourself.
